IV

Section 1 Highlights of 1962 

"This Administration is determined to insure a strong, diversified foundation of healthy small firms; to expand the markets for American enterprises; to preserve a system of free and open competition, and to develop constructive policies and programs in behalf of the small business community." 

-PRESIDENT KENNEDY 

The year 1962 was marked by substantial progress toward achieving the stronger, healthier small business community sought by the President. 

America's 42 million small business concerns were benefited greatly during the year by a continued strong recovery of the national economy from the 1960-61 business recession.

By the close of the year, several key indicators of economic activity had climbed to new highs:

Gross national product was at an annual rate of $562 billion compared with a rate of $538.6 billion in the last quarter of 1961; consumer income had risen from an annual rate of $427.3 billion in the fourth quarter of 1961 to a rate of $448 billion in the same period of 1962; the index of industrial production had moved to a new yearly high of 

118.2 compared with 109.8 in 1961.

This heightened economic activity brought gains to most segments of the small business population. 

With more money to spend, consumers increased their expenditures on durable and nondurable goods and services. The service industries-predominantly a small business field-showed the greatest dollar gain in income, but the Nation's nearly 2 million small retailers also fared well, as retail sales increased about 6 percent over 1961. 

In the manufacturing field, smaller and larger corporations both enjoyed improved sales and earnings in the first three quarters of 1962, compared with a year earlier. The increases were relatively greater for the smaller corporationsparticularly impressive was a jump of 82 percent in their after-taxes earnings.

The position of small business was strengthened further by governmental action to stimulate the economy to a greater rate of growth. Congress enacted a 7 percent tax credit for business expenditures on major kinds of equipment, and the 

Treasury Department adopted new tax depreciation schedules. The measures should be particularly helpful to small firms, making it easier for them to acquire the newer and more modern equipment needed to keep pace with larger competitors.

Small firms benefited also in 1962 from the continuing effort by the Small Business Administration to make its services more helpful to small business. Some highlights of the Agency's activities in the year were: 

Business loans: The SBA approved nearly 

6,000 bank-SBA and direct SBA loans for more than $300 million to help small firms expand, modernize, and improve their operations. The number of loan applications received and number of loans approved were second only to 1961. The 

SBA and the American Bankers Association developed a new bank-SBA participation loan plan which should help greatly to meet the credit needs of small firms. 

Government contracts: Through joint action by SBA and purchasing agencies, a record total of $2.2 billion of proposed Government purchases were set aside for competitive bidding by small firms. The set-aside program helped contribute 1

to the most significant increase in the small business share of military purchasing, and the highest small business share of overall Government purchasing, since fiscal year 1957. In fiscal 1962, contract awards to small firms amounted to 19.3 percent of the total dollar value of all Government contracts and 17.7 percent of the value of military contracts; the respective figures for the previous fiscal year were 17.6 and 15.9 percent. • Small business investment companies: The 

SBA licensed more than 200 additional small business investment companies to provide longterm loans and equity capital to small firms. By the close of the year, the SBIC's had well over a half billion dollars of capital for use in small business loans and investments, and had stepped up their rate of financing small firms. As of the yearend, they had made more than 6,900 small business loans or investments totaling about $360 million. • Disaster loans: The SBA approved about 

3,200 loans to assist victims of storms and similar natural disasters in restoring or replacing their homes, businesses, and other property, or to aid small firms that suffered economic injury from drought or excessive rainfall or through displacement by federally aided construction projects. 

• Community-area development: The agency cooperated with the Area Redevelopment Administration, other Federal agencies, and State and local authorities to help communities establish or expand job-creating industrial and commercial projects. Under its own Small Business Investment Act lending authority, SBA approved more than 100 loans to State and local development companies to aid them in financing small businesses. • Small business management: The SBA intensified its help to small businessmen in overcoming management deficiencies and building stronger, more profitable enterprises. The agency worked with large firms and trade associations, helping them guide their small customers, suppliers, and members toward improved management. The SBA also broadened its programs of management counseling, publications, courses and conferences, and research. • Foreign trade: Because expansion of our foreign trade is vital to the Nation and to small business, SBA accelerated its program of assisting and encouraging small firms in development of markets abroad.

While endeavoring to give all possible service to small business in 1962, SBA also sought increased efficiency in its operations-in short, to provide maximum service at minimum cost. Toward this end, the agency made comprehensive studies of its operating procedures and administration. These studies made possible better and quicker service for small business, higher SBA? productivity, and a reduction in required agency expenditures.

In 1962 also, SBA placed renewed emphasis on its function as spokesman and advocate for small business within the councils of the Federal Government.

The agency strengthened its relationships with other agencies and played a major role in the work of the White House Committee on Small Business. This Committee, appointed by President 

Kennedy, is comprised of representatives of those? departments and agencies within the executive branch of the Federal Government whose activities most directly affect the small business com-s munity. The SBA Administrator serves as 

Chairman. 

As an essential part of its work, the Committee coordinates diverse Federal activities and programs to assist with specific small business problems. For example, the Committee studied carefully the tax problems of small business, then recommended that SBA and the Internal Revenue

Service cosponsor a series of meetings to inform small businessmen of the important tax aspects of their management decisions. The SBA and 

IRS followed up by holding 22 tax managementconferences for small businessmen. Others are planned. 2

Section II 

The Economy and Small Business 

The year 1962 was marked by significant gains in aggregate business activity and the well-being of most of America's consumers. Many key indicators of economic activity reached new peaks during the year. 

Despite these gains, the Nation experienced less than satisfactory economic growth, leading to action by Congress and the national administration to stimulate the economy. 

To encourage investment in new plant and equipment, Congress provided in the Revenue Act of 1962 a 7-percent tax credit for business expenditures on major kinds of equipment. In addition, the Treasury Department adopted basic reforms in depreciation of equipment for tax purposes, making it easier for small firms to accumulate sufficient reserves to buy newer and more modern equipment.

These measures should provide added investment incentives and expanded markets for small business.

Gains Shown by GNP and Other Economic Indicators 

By the fourth quarter of 1962, gross national product had increased to an annual rate of $562 billion, compared with a rate of $538.6 billion in the same quarter of 1961. Personal income rose steadily throughout the year, and reached a seasonally adjusted annual rate of $448 billion by the last quarter, compared with a $427.3 billion rate in the same period of 1961.

As a result of the rise in personal income, including disposable income, consumers increased their expenditures from a seasonally adjusted annual rate of $346.1 billion in the last quarter of 

1961 to a rate of $363.5 billion in the same quarter of 1962. Expenditures on durable and nondurable goods and services all increased, with services registering the greatest dollar gain. Expenditures on durable goods increased to $47.5 billion from $43.7 billion in 1961, and nondurable expenditures to $162 billion from $155.2 billion.

Expenditures on services increased to $147.1 billion from $139.9 billion in 1961. 

An increase in consumer credit outstanding, beginning with the second quarter of 1962, also testified to continuing consumer confidence in the future of the economy.

Retail sales in December totaled an alltime monthly high of $20.2 billion. For the entire year, retail sales were about 6 percent higher than in 1961. Since there are nearly 2 million small retailers, this increase in sales was of substantial benefit to small business.

Manufacturing. In 1962, the index of industrial production reached a new yearly high of 

118.2 compared with 109.8 in 1961 (1957-59 =100). This was a larger gain over 1961 than the previous year's gain over 1960.

Preliminary data show that in both the durable and nondurable goods industries, manufacturers' new orders increased in 1962 as compared with 1961. Manufacturers' sales of durables and nondurables were higher in every month of last year than in the comparable month of 1961. 

During 1962 the average factory workweek totaled 40.4 hours compared with 39.8 hours during 

1961. Average weekly earnings in manufacturing also increased, from $92.30 to $96.60. 

Unemployment. The rate of unemployment dropped to 5.6 percent in 1962 from 6.7 percent in 

1961. However, there are still about 4 million unemployed. If the Nation is to achieve the desired objectives of a greater rate of economic growth and increased use of resources, it must find means to reduce substantially the number of unemployed.

Corporate Profits. Estimates are that corporate profits before taxes totaled approximately $51 billion in 1962-an alltime high-compared 1031042

with $45.5 billion in 1961. The latest available data, covering the first three quarters of 1962, show that sales and profits of manufacturing corporations attained new highs during the year. As detailed later in this section, gains were especially pronounced in the case of corporations with assets of $1 million or less.

Gross Private Domestic Investment. Prelimi nary estimates show that gross private domestic investment in 1962 reached $76.2 billion compared with $69.3 billion in 1961. Despite this increase for the full year, preliminary data show a decline in the fourth quarter compared with the same quarter of 1961. The rate of business investment in inventory declined in the fourth quarter of 1962 by $5.5 billion (at a seasonally adjusted annual rate), offsetting an increase of almost $4 billion in investment in plant and equipment. 

Business Population 

Exceeds 4,800,000

As it had in 1961, the Nation's business population increased last year at a quarterly rate of 10,000 firms. By the end of the fourth quarter of 1962, the population totaled 4,810,000 businesses, of which more than 42 million were small. 

During 1961 a total of 437,000 new businesses were started while 398,000 discontinued for a variety of reasons, resulting in a net increase of nearly 40,000 firms. The largest gains, by major industry divisions, took place in the services industries and in the retail trades, 22,000 and 11,000 firms respectively. Wholesale firms increased by 4,000, while firms in the "All other" category (which includes mining and quarrying, transportation, communications, other public utilities, finance, insurance, and real estate) increased by 10,000.

The distribution of firms by major industry division as of January 1, 1962, shows that the number of firms in the construction industry decreased in 1961 for the first time since 1958. The decline was 4,000 firms. However, the record high construction in 1962, including an increase of over 12 percent in new housing units, undoubtedly was accompanied by an increase in the number of new, small construction firms. 

The only other major industry division to show a decline in 1961 was manufacturing-a drop of 4,000 firms. Not since 1956 has there been a yearly increase in the number of manufacturing firms. 

Over the 10-year period between January 1, 1952, and January 1, 1962, the business population increased by 15 percent-from 4,118,000 to 

4,752,000. The largest relative growth in number of firms occurred in the services industries- -up 24 percent, from 740,000 to 917,000-and in contract construction-up 22 percent, from 387,000 to 473,000. Wholesaling firms also showed a sizable increase-up 18 percent, from 276,000 to 326,000.

The increase in retailing firms was more than 10 percent, from 1,831,000 to 2,022,000. Firms in the "All other" category increased by 25 percent, from 

557,000 to 696,000.

The manufacturing sector showed the only decline by major industry division during the last 10 years a drop of 3 percent, from 328,000 firms to 318,000.

Business Incorporations Rose Last Year 

New business incorporations rose slightly in 1962 the first rise since 1959. New incorporations totaled 182,057 last year compared with 

181,538 in 1961. 

However, during the last 6 months of 1962, new incorporations were 3 percent lower than in the same months of 1961. By comparison, the last half of 1961 had shown a 3.5-percent increase over the same period in 1960.

Passage of the Technical Amendments Act of 

1958, which permits small corporations to be taxed as partnerships, has made the corporate form of organization more popular in recent years. In 

1961, for instance, the number of new business incorporations was over 40 percent of the total number of new businesses formed.

An analysis of new business incorporations by geographic divisions shows that the increase in 1962 occurred in four of the nine geographic divi-

Atlantic, and East South Central-showed a decline of less than 1 percent from 127,979 in 1961 to 126,898 in 1962.

Since 1956 an increasing proportion of new business incorporations has been in the West North Central States (those States immediately west of the Mississippi River), the Mountain

States, and the Southern States. The Pacific region showed the largest relative decline in new business incorporations during this period. The 

Middle Atlantic, East North Central, and New

England regions still account for almost half of all new incorporations.

Unincorporated Enterprises

Show Gain in Income 

Income of unincorporated enterprises in all industrial divisions (excluding agriculture, forestry, and fisheries) rose in 1961 to $34,279 million from $33,759 million in 1960, an increase of 1.5 percent.

Data on 1962 income is not yet available. 

The rise of 1.5 percent in 1961 income does not fully reveal the substantial increase which took place, since much of this gain represented recovery of ground lost during the latter part of 1960. For instance, a closely related measure is proprietors' income, business and professional, which showed a 1.7-percent rise from 1960 to 1961. However, at a seasonally adjusted annual rate proprietors' income, business and professional, rose from $33.7 billion in the first quarter of 1961 to $36 billion in the fourth quarter, an increase of $2.3 billion, or 

6.8 percent.

Available data by industry division show that the unincorporated enterprises which provide services are becoming increasingly important. 

For example, from 1950 to 1961 the income of this group increased from $6,675 million to $12,538 million. As a result, the income of the services group increased from 27 percent of the income of all unincorporated enterprises to 37 percent. 

Business Failures Drop As Economy Recovers 

Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., reported 15,782 business failures in 1962, a decrease of 8 percent from the 

17,075 failures reported in 1961. This was the first yearly decline since 1959, when failures decreased by 6 percent.

The decrease in number of failures was spread widely among the various major industry categories, with the largest percentage decreases occurring among commercial services and the retail trades. These showed decreases of 9 percent each. 

Construction failures reflected the smallest relative decrease, under 2 percent.

Divisions of industry and trades where failures were appreciably fewer in 1962 were: Retail trades—automotive group, furniture and home furnishings, eating and drinking places; manufacturing lumber and lumber products, textile mill products, and apparel and related products; wholesale trades-lumber, building materials and hardware, and motor vehicles and auto equipment; and commercial services-passenger and freight transportation, hotels and laundries.

The only industry divisions to show a notable increase in the number of failures were machinery manufacturing and special trade construction contractors. 

Business Bankruptcies 

Higher in Fiscal 1962

The Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts re ported that 14,336 business bankruptcy cases were filed with the courts in the fiscal year which ended 

June 30, 1962, an increase of about 2 percent from the 14,072 business bankruptcies in fiscal year 1961. 

This compares with an increase of 24 percent between fiscal years 1960 and 1961. The number of business cases filed in each year since fiscal 1953 is shown below, as well as the percent of business bankruptcies to total bankruptcies.

Manufacturers' Sales, Earnings Register Significant Gains 

Sales and earnings of manufacturing corporations showed significant gains in the first three quarters of 1962 compared with the same quarters of 1961. As is generally true in periods of rising economic activity, gains in earnings were relatively greater for the smaller corporations, those with total assets of less than $1 million. Their beforetaxes earnings increased by 47 percent, their aftertaxes earnings by 82 percent.

Sales. The smaller corporations' sales in the first three quarters of last year increased to $37,670 million from $34,997 million in the same period of 1961, a gain of almost 8 percent. Sales of the smaller corporations in the third quarter of 1962 totaled $13,005 million, compared with $12,035 million in the like quarter of 1961.

Sales of the larger manufacturing corporations (those with assets over $1 million) in the first three quarters of 1962 totaled $250,318 million, compared with $226,273 million in the same quarters of 1961, a gain of almost 11 percent. In the third quarter of 1962, sales of the larger corporations reached $83,099 million compared with $76,796 million in the third quarter of 1961. 

Before-Taxes Earnings. Earnings before taxes of the smaller corporations in the first three quarters of 1962 increased to $1,342 million from $910 million in the comparable period of 1961, a gain of 47 percent. Third-quarter 1962 earnings increased to $540 million from $405 million in the same quarter of 1961. 

Before-taxes earnings of the larger manufacturing corporations during the first three quarters of 1962 rose to $22,191 million from $18,464 million in the comparable period of 1961, an increase of 20 percent. Included in these totals were thirdquarter 1962 earnings of $7,049 million and thirdquarter 1961 earnings of $6,439 million. 

After-Taxes Earnings. Earnings after taxes also increased sharply during the first three quarters of 1962 compared with the same period of 1961. 

Earnings after taxes of smaller manufacturing corporations increased to $750 million from $411 million in the comparable period of 1961, a gain of 82 percent. Their earnings after taxes in the third quarter of 1962 rose to $318 million from $215 million in the third quarter of 1961.

Larger corporations' earnings after taxes increased in the first three quarters of 1962 to $12,126 million from $10,291 million in the same period of 1961, a gain of 18 percent. Their third-quarter earnings after taxes increased to $3,905 million from $3,622 million in the same quarter of 1961. 

Return on Stockholders' Equity. Earnings on stockholders' equity, both before and after taxes, reflected the substantial increases in earnings of manufacturing corporations in the first three quarters of 1962.

Before taxes, the average annual rate of return for smaller corporations increased to 16.7 percent from 11.9 percent in the first three quarters of 

1961. After taxes, the smaller manufacturing corporations' earnings on stockholders' equity in the first three quarters of 1962 increased to 9.3 percent from 5.4 percent in the same period of 1961.

For the larger manufacturing corporations, the annual rate of return before taxes averaged 17.4 percent compared with 15.3 percent in the first three quarters of 1961. Their after-tax rate increased to 9.5 percent from 8.5 percent.

As shown in the chart on page 13, the annual rate of return for the second and third quarters of 1962, both before and after taxes, was higher for the smaller manufacturing corporations than for the larger corporations. 

Small Increase in Mergers

Reported by FTC Last Year 

Data on mergers is published by the Bureau of 

Economics, Federal Trade Commission, which lists acquisitions reported under negotiation in "Moody's Industrials," "Standard Corporation

Records," and selected trade and daily newspapers. The FTC reports are published for the sole purpose of following the trend in the merger movement and should not be considered as presenting firm figures on completed mergers, nor as including all acquisitions that may have been under negotiation.

The following table shows the trend in the merger movement for the years 1955 through 1962.

In recorded mergers increased by 2 percent over 1961. However, this is a considerable decline in the rate of growth of the merger movement when compared with the increase in mergers in 1961. 

Discontinuances because of mergers, although proportionately few in number, represent a trend which is of vital concern to small business. Some mergers and acquisitions are, of course, desirable. 

This is particularly true where two or three smaller businesses join to form a new business enterprise of sufficient competitive strength to either maintain or increase their combined position in the market. On the other hand, many mergers and acquisitions of small firms by big corporations weaken the competitive position of small business.

Concern over merger activity is evidenced by a recent staff report of the House Select Committee on Small Business which focuses attention on the merger actions of leading corporations during the past decade. This study lists reported acquisitions of the 500 largest industrial corporations and the 50 largest merchandising firms. 

Between 1951 and 1961, the 500 largest industrials acquired some 3,400 other companies. The study reveals that a few industries have been most active in the merger movement and that in these industries most of the leading firms have made numerous mergers. For example, 1,650 acquisitions of the total 3,400 recorded for the 500 largest corporations were accounted for by eight industries. These acquisitions occurred in dairy products, paper and allied products, industrial chemicals, petroleum refining, aerospace, electrical equipment, motor vehicles, and textilemill products.

Small Business Act Calls for SBA Studies The Small Business Act directs SBA, as part of its responsibility to assist and preserve small firms, to "make studies of matters materially affecting the competitive strength of small business, and of the effect on small business of Federal laws, programs, and regulations. . 99 The SBA carries out this responsibility through research studies developed by the agency itself or by research organizations under contract to SBA. These studies inform the agency of the dynamics of economics, business, and Government affecting small business. They supply data needed by SBA and other agencies in formulating Federal policies affecting small business.

They deal with the competitive conditions of small business and with factors bearing on opportunities of small companies their failures and successes, their decline and growth.

During 1962 the Small Business Administration completed for publication two of several reports being prepared on the competitive position of smaller companies in the manufacturing industries. 

"Size Structure of Manufacturing Industries in the United States, 1958," presents statistics on the number of relatively smaller companies in each industry and the percent of value added by manufacture accounted for by these companies. 

The second study of the manufacturing industries complements the industry study and is developed from a commodity approach. It is concerned with the competitive position of smaller companies in the production of the same or substitutable groups of products. It takes into account the number and economic importance of smaller companies in the production of classes of products despite differences in the companies' principal manufacturing activities or in processes and materials used.

These studies are based on statistical tabulations of the 1958 Census of Manufactures compiled especially for SBA by the Bureau of the Census. 

Each report provides information not published previously on the size structure of companies engaged in manufacturing. 

During 1962, the International Marketing Institute, a nonprofit foundation for education and research at the Harvard Graduate School of Business Administration, completed the basic work on a study of small business in the export market. This manual, "Export Marketing for

Smaller Firms," was prepared under contract to SBA, and will be issued about May 1963. The study outlines the information needed by a small business in determining whether to export and the problems involved in entering the export field. 

It also gives sources of information on foreign trade and step-by-step procedures to follow in entering the export field. The study is the first of its kind and should prove an invaluable aid to potential exporters and those who counsel them. 

Temple University, Philadelphia, Pa., has completed the final draft of a study being done under contract to SBA. In this study the university 10 14

is developing financial and operating ratios from dollar data reported by the Internal Revenue

Service in its unpublished source book of statistics of income.

By the end of 1962, SBA had done substantial work on a third study of the manufacturing industries, to be published in 1963. This study is concerned with changes in the competitive position of smaller companies in each manufacturing industry between the years 1954 and 1958. 

Plans also had been made for publication of several studies dealing with the effect on small business of specific Federal programs. 

Dunlap & Associates, Inc., Stamford, Conn., under a contract with SBA, had begun work on a study of small businesses which received SBA loan disbursements from July 1, 1956 through 

June 30, 1959.

A preliminary survey to determine the feasibility of measuring small business participation in a Department of Defense weapons system program had been completed for SBA by Dynatech Corp. of Cambridge, Mass. The advisability of undertaking a larger study of this subject was under consideration.

In the planning stage was a study of criteria to be used in determining the need for and saturation levels of SBA-licensed small business investment companies in given geographical areas. 

Another study upon which research has begun concerns the development of small business in the 

Pacific Northwest. The University of Washing ton is evaluating the competitive effects of the structure of industry upon the growth of small concerns. 15

Section III Financing Small Firms 

Difficulty in obtaining adequate financing on reasonable terms continued to be a major problem of small business in 1962. The SBA helped with the problem through counseling on financial matters, assistance in obtaining private financing, loans in participation with banks, and direct 

Government loans.

During the year SBA approved nearly 6,000 loans to help small firms strengthen their operations, expand and modernize their facilities, and increase job opportunities in their communities. 

The agency also introduced a new loan plan designed to encourage additional banks to join with 

SBA in loans to small businesses.

Business Loan Applications, 

Approvals at High Level

Business loan activity in 1962 was the second highest of any year in the history of SBA. The agency received 11,245 loan applications and approved 5,953 loans totaling $316,021,000. These totals are exceeded only by the 1961 figures of 

13,680 applications and 6,836 loans for $369,414,000.

Through December 31, 1962, SBA had approved a cumulative total of 35,072 business loans for $1,736,888,000.

SBA Financial Counseling Aids Small Firm Owners, Managers 

During the past year, SBA counseled nearly 

8,300 small businessmen on specific financial problems.

In counseling a businessman, SBA financial specialists first pinpoint his problems, then recommend possible solutions. Often, problems are resolved without need of borrowing. However, if borrowing appears to be the answer, SBA helps the businessman seek financing from private lending institutions. When necessary, SBA will consider participating in a loan with private sources. 

If none of the needed funds can be obtained from private sources, SBA will consider making a direct Government loan to the small business. 

The agency's program of financial assistance is designed to supplement the lending activities of commercial banks, not compete with them. Therefore, SBA always strives for bank participation, and one of its principal objectives is increased cooperation with banks in meeting the term-credit needs of small businesses. The new loan plan discussed in the following section, together with the agency's other participation plans, should help to achieve this objective.

New Simplified Loan Plan Stimulates Bank Participation 

In September, SBA inaugurated a Simplified 

Bank Loan Plan With Early Maturity Feature. 

It is another step in the agency's continuing program to encourage bank participation in loans. 

The new plan was developed jointly by SBA and the American Bankers Association. Through it, SBA and the ABA are cooperating to provide qualified small businesses with term loans from their primary source of credit, commercial banks. 

The plan offers a bank several benefits. The early maturity feature permits the bank to be repaid its share of a loan before repayment of SBA's 16

share begins. The bank must agree to take at least a 50-percent share of the loan and participate for at least half the life of the loan. For example, a bank will be repaid in the first 5 years if it takes a 50-percent participation in a 10-year loan; and in 6 years if it takes a 60-percent interest in a 10year loan. The bank has the option to purchase 

SBA's share of the loan at any time. All loans under the new plan are "immediate participation loans"; that is, at the bank's request SBA will advance its share of the loan as soon as the bank is ready to disburse funds.

In applying for a loan under the plan, the small businessman deals entirely with his bank and relies on the bank for guidance in preparing the necessary credit information. The bank then submits the credit information to SBA with a request that

SBA participate in the loan. The bank uses its own procedures and forms (except for SBA's note) in closing the loan, and submits copies of the loan documents to SBA only after disbursing the loan. The bank also agrees to service the loan. 

Small Firms Benefit From 

Increased Bank Participation 

Nearly 66 percent of the loans approved by

SBA in 1962 were of the bank-participation type, compared with 60 percent in 1961.

The increase in bank participation is particularly gratifying, because SBA believes that lending to businesses preferably should be carried out at the local level, either by, or in close cooperation with, the Nation's banks. In furtherance of this policy, all SBA loan applications are accepted and processed in the agency's field offices. These offices have been given broad authority to approve business loans, so that relatively few applications are sent to Washington for final action.

During 1962, commercial banks participated with SBA in 3,910 loans. These loans amounted to $244 million, or more than 77 percent of the dollar amount of loan approvals in the year.

SBA Business Loans Aid Other Federal Programs 

Many of the business loans made by SBA help to further the programs of other Federal agencies. 

For example, SBA makes loans to hospitals and convalescent and nursing homes which are privately owned and operated for profit. These loans are of particular interest to the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare and the Fed eral Housing Administration. Similarly, SBA 

loans to finance the construction of aircraft stor--age hangars for rental purposes contribute to the programs of the Federal Aviation Agency; SBA loans for access-road construction by small purchasers of timber from national forests aid in the work of the Departments of Interior and Agriculture; and SBA loans to small business projects in redevelopment areas (see sec. VI) further the goals of the Area Redevelopment Administration. 

SBA Strives for More 

Effective Aid to Borrowers

After making a loan, SBA does not lose interest in the borrower and its financial progress. The agency also tries to see to it that the SBA loan proves of maximum help.

Where a loan is not. serviced by a participating bank, it is administered by the SBA field office in the borrower's area. There, specialists trained in financial counseling and analysis of financial statements regularly review the borrower's loan record and the financial reports he submits. 

Through this review, they are able to evaluate the small firm's progress. The specialists also maintain close personal contact with the firms whose loans they service. They visit the borrowing businesses and give special attention to finding and solving the problems of those which are experiencing difficulties.

Of course, SBA's responsibility to assist small business is matched by its responsibility to guard the investment of taxpayers' funds. Therefore, if the financial condition of a borrower warrants it, SBA acts promptly to protect its position as a lender. 

Analyses of actual and projected losses on 

SBA's business and disaster loans will be found in the section of this report dealing with the SBA revolving fund (p. 64). 

Loan Policies, Procedures 

Undergo Constant Review

The general loan policies of the Small Business Administration are determined by a Loan Policy Board comprised of the Secretaries of the Treasury and Commerce and the Administrator of the

SBA, who serves as Chairman. The Board revises SBA's loan policies whenever this is necessary to meet the changing needs of small business and of the national economy as a whole. 20

Beyond this, the agency's loan procedures are under continuing review by operating officials to assure prompt and sympathetic consideration of loan applications. Staff members of SBA's Office of Loan Processing in Washington regularly visit the agency's regional and branch offices to review their lending operations. This results in a more uniform application of loan policies and procedures and greater efficiency in loan matters. Postauditing of loan cases decided by field offices under delegated authority has helped to achieve these same objectives.

In addition, SBA's lending operations are reviewed by the agency's Office of Audits, Office of 

Program Analysis, and Office of Organization and Management. Staff members of these offices may make comments, criticisms, and recommendations as to all SBA operations or programs.

New Loan Program 

Authorized in 1962

The Trade Expansion Act of 1962 (Public Law 

87-794) and a 1962 amendment to the Small Business Act (Public Law 87-550) authorize SBA loans to help businesses of any size adjust to changed economic conditions resulting from increased competition from imported articles, provided the Secretary of Commerce certifies that the firms are eligible for adjustment assistance. This 

SBA financial assistance program has not yet been placed in operation, but tentative operating procedures have been developed within SBA and in conjunction with the Department of Commerce. 21

A timely SBA loan to this work glove manufacturer in Tennessee saved 75 jobs and helped maintain normal company operations. The small firm is located in a marginal employment area and today employs over 200 persons in an essential industry.

An SBA loan helped this firm to expand its volume and increase employment in a northern Minnesota area by providing working capital and equipment.

Modern tourist accommodations are provided by this 100-unit hotel built in Puerto Rico with the help of financing from SBA and a bank. 

A medical clinic constructed with the aid of a bank-SBA loan provides medical and specialty care for a five-county area of western Kentucky. Together with a neighboring affiliated clinic, it gives direct employment to about 70 people in an area of substantial unemployment. 

A direct SBA loan of $145,000 enabled a northern Wisconsin community to obtain this fine resort lodge to provide increased facilities for the tourist trade in the heart of the area's vacationland.

SBA loan.

SBA DISASTER LOANS Help Rehabilitate Communities 

Laid Waste by Nature's Storms...

ARCHORAGE 

When tidal waves, tornadoes, floods or other natural disasters strike America's communities, SBA specialists move quickly to the stricken areas to provide long-term, low interest loans to rehabilitate businesses, homes and non-profit institutions. Last March's three-day storm inflicted millions of dollars of damage to the Mid-Atlantic Coast as shown in the accompanying photographs. SBA helped with the rebuilding job by approving 1,293 disaster loans totaling more than $12 million--nearly one-half the number of disaster loans approved by the Agency last year for the entire country. Photos show extensive damage sustained at Virginia Beach and Atlantic City. Lower picture depicts Virginia Beach rebuilt and ready for a boom season.

Section IV 

Aiding Victims of Disasters 

An important responsibility of the Small Busiess Administration is to make loans to assist victims of various types of disasters-those whose roperty has been damaged by storms or other atural disasters, and those whose small businesses ave suffered economic injury from drought or xcessive rainfall, or from displacement by fedrally aided urban renewal or other construction programs.

Physical Damage Loans 

Are Broadest Activity 

The SBA's oldest and most extensive disaster ssistance program involves loans to those whose property has been damaged or destroyed by floods, torms, or other natural catastrophes. 

These loans are made in areas designated by the 

Administrator of SBA.

Loans are made to individuals, business conerns (regardless of size), and nonprofit organiations such as churches and charitable instituions to repair or replace tangible property loss caused by the disaster.

There is no statutory limit on the amount of a oan; the limit is determined by the tangible net oss suffered by an applicant, less any insurance benefits. The SBA charges 3 percent interest on loan made entirely by the agency or on the gency's share of a loan made in participation with a bank.

Economic Injury Loans Aid Small Businesses 

In its other disaster assistance programs, SBA makes loans to small firms that have suffered economic loss because of drought or excessive rainfall or displacement by federally aided construction programs.

Drought and Excessive Rainfall Loans. These loans are made to small businesses in areas designated by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture. Loans may be of the participation or direct type and may be used for working capital, to replenish normal inventories, and for debt payment (except bank loans) which the borrower would have been able to meet had it not been for the loss of revenue due to the drought or excessive rainfall. These loans, like physical-damage loans, bear interest of 3 percent on SBA's share.

Displaced Business Loans. Under an amend ment to the Small Business Act included in the Housing Act of 1961, SBA makes loans to assist small firms which are forced to relocate because of federally aided urban renewal, highway, and other construction programs. The SBA's assistance is in addition to compensation for moving expenses or direct loss of property which the firm may receive from the local public agency conducting the renewal project.

The SBA loans help small businesses reestablish themselves in suitable and reasonable locations. 

The loans may be made in participation with banks or directly by SBA. The interest rate on 

SBA's share is established annually on a statutory formula based on the average interest rate on all interest-bearing obligations of the United 

States plus one-quarter of 1 percent. The current rate is 312 percent. 

More Than 3,000

Loans Approved 

In 1962, SBA received 3,703 disaster loan applications and approved 3,155 for $33,641,000. (Approvals included some applications which had been pending at the start of the year.) 

Of the loans approved, 3,002 for $26,265,000 were physical-damage loans; 11 for $155,000 were drought loans; 28 for $444,000 were excessive rainfall loans; and 114 for $6,777,000 were displaced-business loans.

Through the end of 1962 SBA had approved a cumulative total of 18,910 disaster loans of all types for $169,866,000. 27

Section V 

Providing Long-Term Financing For Small 

Business 

A milestone in the Small Business Investment

Company Program was reached in October 1962, when the one-thousandth proposal to operate an

SBIC was filed with the Small Business Administration. By year's end, the number of proposals had grown to 1,013.

The surge of new proposals reached a peak in 1961, and gradually declined following the early months of 1962. Two factors contributed to the decline. First, the stock market slump in May 

1962, had a major impact, as private funds for investment in the program were not readily available. Second, SBA's own funds were severely limited pending action on the agency's budget request. As a result, SBA found it necessary to hold up approval of commitments to assist newly organized SBIC's until funds were made available.

Despite the dropoff in proposals, the rate of licensing new SBIC's was only moderately below that of 1961, and was almost double the rate of 1960 (see chart). By the end of 1962, SBA had granted licenses to a total of 664 SBIC's. At - December 31, after deducting licenses canceled, 643 SBIC's were in operation, based in 42 States, the District of Columbia, Puerto Rico, and the

Virgin Islands. Their approved areas of operation included every State. 

Funds Available for Financing

Small Business Continue To Rise 

Funds available to SBIC's for investment in small business concerns continued a steady rise. 

At the end of 1962, the 643 SBIC's had $553 million in capitalization, 28 percent higher than at the end of 1961, when the total was $432 million. 

The increase of $121 million during the year - was accounted for by: 681420 0-63- -3

SBA for capital purposes-- Less decrease resulting from surrender of 15 licenses Million $33 19 49 42 143 22 121 Most of the $33 million in new public offerings was obtained in the early months of 1962, before the stock market decline. In contrast, the additional $19 million invested in the program by private sources was spread throughout the year. 

Most of these funds were subscribed by individuals who already were shareholders of SBIC's, and who thus evidenced their confidence in the future of the industry in the most concrete wayby investing more of their own money in it. 

The SBIC's total capitalization of $553 million, as of the close of 1962, included net SBA commitments or disbursements, in return for subordinated debentures, of $98.8 million. Thus, the ratio of private capital funds to capital funds supplied by SBA was nearly 5 to 1. 

Late in 1961, the Small Business Investment

Act was amended to increase from $150,000 to $400,000 the amount that SBA can lend an SBIC through purchase of subordinated debentures. 

Funds were to be made available for both formation and growth purposes, and on a matchingdollar basis with funds obtained by an SBIC from private sources.

The SBA set $150,000 as the maximum amount it would provide to help form a company, and ruled that it would purchase an additional $250,- 

000 of debentures for "growth" purposes only after the company had invested at least 75 per- 29

200 125 100 219 499 154 16 1958 1959 1960 1961 1962 cent of its assets. During 1962, SBA approved the purchase of $21 million of debentures of 92 

SBIC's to provide funds for growth purposes.

Late in the year, the agency amended its regulations to provide that it would match private funds up to $400,000 immediately upon formation of a company. This eliminated the requirement that an SBIC must have invested substantially all its assets before becoming eligible to borrow the additional $250,000.

Bank Guarantee Loan Plan Aids in Financing SBIC's 

The Small Business Investment Act authorizes SBA to lend an SBIC operating funds totaling up to 50 percent of the company's statutory capital and surplus, or $4 million, whichever is the lesser. 

In September 1961, SBA initiated a plan designed to encourage banks to participate with the agency in operating loans to SBIC's. The plan provides for the granting of a loan by SBA and immediate assignment of the loan to a bank under an agreement whereby the bank can deliver the note to SBA at any time and obtain the outstanding principal balance.

In an action that was important to SBA financing of SBIC's, the Comptroller General of the

United States ruled in September 1962 that this "standby" plan developed by SBA was not inconsistent with the intent of the Small Business 

Investment Act. 

During 1962, SBA approved 70 loans for $15.3 million under the bank standby plan. In 1961 it had approved two loans for $2 million. The 196162 total of $17.3 million of loans was disbursed by 

41 banks, including 5 of the 10 largest banks in the country. At the end of the year, SBA had on hand 17 additional loan applications totaling nearly $4 million.

In addition to loans under the bank-disbursed plan, SBA approved 73 direct loans amounting to $13.5 million last year. From the start of the program through the close of 1962, the agency had approved 146 direct loans totaling $30 million. Twenty other applications totaling $4.6 million were in process at year's end. 

SBIC's Increase Their

Small Firm Investments 

As of the yearend, 606 small business investment companies had reported making 6,937 small business loans and investments totaling $360 million. 

NEW SBIC LICENSES APPROVED 

300 200 100 NUMBER 62 113 273 216 1959 1960 1961 1962 30

Reports from 364 reporting SBIC's as of 

September 30, 1961, had shown about 2,700 loan or investment transactions totaling $152.2 million, or 40.3 percent of the companies' total assets at that date. On September 30, 1962, the 565 SBIC's reported $307.8 million invested through about 

5,000 transactions. The investments constituted 

55.5 percent of the SBIC's total assets at that date.

It is interesting to note that the dispersion of investments was very stable over the Septemberto-September period. As of September 30, 1961, gross loans and investments were distributed 36 percent to loans, 52 percent to debt securities, and 12 percent to capital stock of small business concerns. As of September 30, 1962, the loans constituted 37 percent, the debt securities 52 percent, and the capital stock 11 percent.

As the SBIC's have become more mature and have devoted a relatively higher percentage of their assets to loans and investments, a parallel increase has occurred in their total income in relation to total operating expenses. The combined statements of 364 SBIC's showed, for the second and third quarters of 1961, gross income of $6.4 million and total operating expenses of $3.9 million. In the comparable period of 1962, the 565 SBIC's showed gross income of $14.1 million and total operating expenses of $7.8 million. 

Regulatory Changes Strengthen SBIC's Financial Security 

SBA regulations governing small business investment companies were amended in 1962 to give them more freedom of action in their operations and at the same time to require better control of funds and securities and adequate insurance coverage.

In one change, SBIC's were permitted to place idle operating funds in time certificates of deposit maturing in 6 months. (Early in 1963 this period was extended to 1 year.) SBIC's may also participate in mergers; purchase, sell, and exchange portfolio securities among one another; and use custodians of portfolio securities not located in the same city. Also, where several SBIC's participate in financing a small business, the dollar limit on an individual SBIC's participation has been raised from $300,000 to $500,000. 

The amendments strengthening financial security require dual control over disbursement of funds and withdrawal of securities from safekeeping. They also set the minimum amount of the

A recodification of the rules and regulations embodying all the amendments was published in the 

Federal Register in October and distributed to the investment companies.

Licenses Surrendered by 

15 SBIC's in 1962

During the year, 15 SBIC's surrendered their licenses, making a cumulative total of 21 that have gone out of business. These 21 surrenders have taken $25 million out of the total capitalization of the program. However, the SBIC's had invested a considerable part of this amount in small business concerns prior to surrendering their licenses.

There were three major reasons for the surrenders. One, the SBIC wanted to do things which SBA regulations would not permit, such as investing in a large business, buying listed securities, or acquiring a majority interest in small business; two, inactivity as a result of disagreement in management about what kind of investments should be made; and three, developments in the directors' other business interests which pre- 31

cluded devoting the necessary time to the operation of the SBIC.

Data Processing Speeds Reporting Procedures 

During 1962 SBA began electronic processing of financial data furnished the agency by SBIC's. 

This speeded up publication of combined statements of the companies' financial condition, income, and expense. The new processing procedure was first used in summarizing SBIC financial data as of March 31, 1962, and later at the end of June and the end of September.

Summaries are now published within approximately 2 months following the close of the period to which they relate. This enables the small business investment companies to obtain current and comprehensive information on overall investment and operating activities of the companies as a group, and also by size and age classifications. 

Analytical Review of

Combined Statements 

In publishing the quarterly financial summaries of SBIC operations, the agency now places increased emphasis on the analysis and interpretation of operating and investment data. Each summary now includes a covering memorandum relating to effective management of the investment companies, based on the accompanying data. This information serves as a guide to company management in improving their operations.

Audits and Examinations Of SBIC's Are Broadened 

SBIC's are required by the Small Business In-E vestment Act and SBA regulations to be audited annually by independent public accountants approved by the agency. The SBA's own examiners also examine the records of the companies, primarily to determine compliance with the act and regulations.

Under the agency's examination program an SBIC will be examined, on the average, once in each 2-year period. The examined companies pay a large share of the cost of the SBA examinations, and payments are deposited into SBA's revolving fund in the U.S. Treasury.

The SBA conducted 98 such examinations in 1962. By the end of the year, it had reported its findings to 82 of the companies, and work was progressing on the remaining 16 reports. 175

Improved SBA Operations And Administration 

The SBA substantially improved the SBIC program administration in 1962. 

For example, investment division chiefs in the agency's regional offices now check SBIC's which are delinquent in filing the required quarterly financial report. When the Washington office issues a delinquency notice, a copy is sent to the SBA field representative, who in turn calls on the delinquent SBIC at the earliest possible date. The number of reports now outstanding at publication deadlines has been reduced substantially. 

A mailing list of about 500 public accountants engaged in auditing and examining SBIC's was established during the year. This mailing list assures the accountants prompt receipt of SBA accounting and auditing items necessary to their work, as well as other information on the SBIC program.

Adequacy and Uniformity in Fidelity Bonds. 

SBA regulations were amended in 1962 to effect a change in minimum fidelity bond requirements for SBIC's and to stipulate the types of bonds and endorsements or riders which would be acceptable to SBA. All SBIC's were notified by means of the regulation amendment itself and a memorandum listing the specific changes. Copies of the amendment were provided the Surety Association of America for distribution to member surety companies. 33

Section VI Assisting With Community Development 

Each Small Business Administration service to small business-financing, help in selling to the Government, management assistance, and so forth-contributes to business prosperity and expansion and resulting community growth. 

In many instances, these SBA services are provided small firms located in areas that need additional employment, and help to create jobs and stimulate overall economic activity.

In addition, however, SBA has several programs which are specifically designed to raise the economic level of States and communities to help them expand their established industries and add new ones, and in this way increase markets for their products, and jobs and income for their citizens.

These programs include SBA loans to State and local development companies for use in financing small businesses, and SBA cooperation in the local-State-Federal Government program to redevelop areas of chronic and substantial unemployment and underemployment.

Development Loan Activity 

Increased Sharply in 1962

Under the Small Business Investment Act, SBA 

may make loans to State and local development companies for use in helping specific small businesses to expand, modernize, or diversify, or to help new small firms get started. The agency also may lend a State development company as much as its total outstanding borrowings from all other sources, for the general purpose of providing long-term loans and equity financing to small businesses.

Demand for development company loans increased sharply in 1962, continuing an uptrend which began after Congress amended the Small 

Business Investment Act in 1961 to liberalize loans to assist specific small businesses. The maximum dollar amount of the loans was increased from $250,000 to $350,000, and the maximum maturity from 10 to 25 years. The changes stimulated greater interest in the program because they made it possible for small firms to acquire needed facilities without the burdensome payments required under the previous 10-year loan term. 

Also contributing to the heightened interest in the program last year was action taken by SBA in 1961 to give increased help to areas suffering from substantial unemployment. The agency reduced the interest rate on development company loans in areas of substantial unemployment to 4 percent, compared with its usual rate of 5 or 5½ percent. Moreover, it increased by 25 percent the size limit on firms in these areas that may be assisted with SBA loans.

The helpful effect of these actions is evident in the fact that SBA now has approved more than 60 loans totaling $8.8 million in these areas. 

Local Development Company Loans. In 1962, 

SBA approved 100 loans for $14.1 million to help local development companies assist small busi- nesses.

The 1962 totals compare with cumulative approvals of 260 loans, for $33.7 million, since the start of the program in August 1958.

The small business projects which SBA helped to finance last year through local development company loans will provide employment for about 

7,200 persons. On a cumulative basis, SBA's loans to local development companies provided employment for some 15,700 people by the end of 1962. 

The local development company loan program has been particularly beneficial to the economy of rural communities-the vast majority of the loans have gone to communities with fewer than 15,000 people. Many of these communities had suffered heavy unemployment because of decreasing opportunities in agriculture. The SBA's development company loans have made it possible for them to diversify their economy and create new jobs. 34

State Development Company Loans. In 1962, 

SBA received 4 applications from State development companies for loans to be used for the general purpose of financing small business, rather than to assist particular firms. Three loans totaling $1,630,000 were approved, and one application for $500,000 was pending as of the close of the year.

Cumulatively through the end of 1962, SBA had approved 12 loans to State development companies for general small business financing purposes.

The loans totaled $8.7 million. 

SBA Amends Regulation On Business Relocations The Small Business Investment Act provides that an SBA loan to a development company must not result in a substantial increase of unemployment in any area of the country. 

In furtherance of this policy, during 1962 SBA amended its regulations to provide that where a development company loan involves the relocation of a small business, a substantial increase in unemployment will be presumed to occur if (a) the relocation would result in unemployment for onethird or more of the work force of the small business; (b) the unemployment would result in the affected area becoming an area of substantial unemployment as designated by the Department of Labor; (c) the area already has been so designated by the Labor Department; or (d) the area has been designated a redevelopment area by the Secretary of Commerce pursuant to the Area Redevelopment Act. 

SBA Playing Major Role

In Area Redevelopment The SBA continued to work closely throughout the year with the Area Redevelopment Administration (ARA) of the Department of Commerce, with other Federal agencies, and with State and local authorities in a joint effort to establish and expand industries and commerce in areas designated by ARA for redevelopment. 

Under delegation from ARA the Small Business 

Administration plays a major role in this program to assist areas of substantial and persistent unemployment. The SBA investigates for ARA the feasibilities of industrial and commercial projects; negotiates agreements for their financing; proc-

Where a small business project needs money for purposes for which ARA funds cannot be used (for working capital, for example), and the funds are also unavailable from a State or local source or another Federal agency, SBA may provide the needed financing through its business loan program.

The SBA also may help to finance small business projects under its development company loan program.

The planning, programing, staffing and training required for the performance of its area redevelopment functions were intensified by SBA in 1962, in particular through training sessions attended by SBA and ARA personnel in nine cities.

During the year there was growing use of SBA- ARA technical services at earlier stages in project planning, resulting in a broadening and strengthening of the financial structure of projects and more rapid processing of applications for ARA loans.

In 1962, SBA studied the feasibility of 314 projects and conducted financial analyses resulting in ARA's approval of 102 loans aggregating $26.4 million. The SBA itself made working capital loans to sponsors of 16 projects, and in one case loaned a development company $99,000 to construct a facility for a small business. The agency fully disbursed 20 ARA loans for about $3 million, and partially disbursed funds on 21 others as needed. In the second half of the year alone, SBA accepted 191 projects for analysis. At yearend, 174 projects were in various review stages, with new ones being added at a rate of 40 per month. 38

Section VII Helping Small Firms Get Government Contracts One of the Small Business Administration's ajor responsibilities is to help small firms obtain larger share of the Federal Government's conracts, both as prime contractors and subconractors.

Highlights of the assistance given small firms n 1962 were: 

1. The SBA and Government purchasing gencies jointly set aside or reserved a record $2.2 illion of purchases to be made exclusively from mall business. This helped to reverse a downvard trend in contract awards to small firms. 

2. The SBA obtained greater opportunity for mall firms to compete for specific contracts, suplied the names of many small suppliers on request of contracting agencies, and helped these gencies open up numerous "sole-source" purchases -o bidding by small business. 

3. Through meetings with representatives of major prime contractors and buying agencies,

SBA officials laid the groundwork for a strengthened program of subcontract assistance to small business.

Set-Asides for Small Firms 

Reached Record Level in 1962 

Last year, SBA and Government purchasing gencies jointly set aside 44,174 proposed purhases valued at $2.2 billion for competitive bidling by small firms. These set-asides resulted in small firms being awarded 54,179 contracts totalng $1.8 billion. In 1961, SBA and purchasing gencies jointly reserved for small business 41,016 proposed purchases valued at $2 billion; they resulted in 45,856 contract awards totaling $1.5 billion. 

The 23.5-percent increase in dollar value of setside awards to small firms in 1962 reflects the improved and growing cooperation between SBA and the military and civilian purchasing agencies.

The set-aside program has proved particularly effective in regard to military purchasing, helping to reverse a downward trend in contract awards to small firms. In the fiscal year which ended 

June 30, 1962 (military procurement is reported on a fiscal rather than calendar year basis), the small business share of contracts was 17.7 percent, compared with 15.9 percent in fiscal year 1961.

This is the most significant increase in military contract awards to small firms since 1957. The dollar amount of small business awards increased from $3.7 billion in fiscal 1961 to $4.6 billion in fiscal 1962. 

Shortly after assuming office, President 

Kennedy directed the Defense Department to increase its purchases from small business. In 

October 1962, he asked the General Services Administration, the Atomic Energy Commission, and the National Aeronautics and Space Ad ministration to take similar action to increase small business awards. The SBA is working closely with each of these agencies to help them meet the President's request.

The SBA's joint set-aside program with purchasing agencies began on August 1, 1953. Since then, it has resulted in small firms being awarded 

205,594 contracts totaling $7.4 billion. Awards have increased in value from $161,000 in calendar year 1954 to $1.8 billion in 1962.

Additional data on the set-aside program is presented later in this section under "Small Business Share of Government Purchasing."

How the Program Helps Small Firms. In a typical case, the Air Force and SBA agreed to reserve for small business an entire purchase of aircraft wing tanks. Six small firms submitted acceptable bids. The award was made to the lowest bidder at a price of approximately $2.2 million.

In another instance, a purchase of nine coastal minesweepers was set aside for small business by 39

the Navy and SBA. The SBA representative at the buying installation then asked SBA offices to notify potential small business bidders. As a result, seven small firms bid on the contract. The purchase was made from two of the bidders, with one receiving a contract for almost $7 million and the other a contract for more than $6 million. 

SBA Finds Additional Small Business Bidders 

SBA representatives at major buying centers continually review proposed purchases for setaside and related purposes. Whenever they find purchases that small firms can supply but that few or none have bid on in the past, they seek additional bidders.

By increasing small business competition in this way, SBA helps obtain a better price on Government purchases. It also helps to broaden the base of Government suppliers and to strengthen small business.

During 1962 SBA worked closely with the De partment of Defense to increase contract awards to small firms in areas of substantial unemployment. The Department first screened all contracts with an estimated value over $500,000 to single out those which might be performed in areas needing additional employment. The SBA then furnished the names of small firms located in these areas that were interested in bidding. 

SBA Advises, Assists

With Prime, Subcontracts 

The SBA advises and assists small firms in obtaining Government prime contracts and subcontracts, helping them to learn which agencies or prime contractors buy the products or services they offer and guiding them in developing opportunities to bid. The SBA also helps small firms resolve problems arising out of contracts with 

Government agencies and prime contractors.

In addition to assisting through its field offices the SBA helps through a Procurement Advisory Center located in Washington. This center provides businessmen current information on military agency invitations for bids and requests for proposals. The center also maintains abstracts of recent bids received by the Army, Air Force, and 

Defense Supply Agency. 

A typical example of SBA assistance of this type involved a small business whose orders dropped steeply when one of its principal customers cut back production. Unless new work could be found quickly, the firm would be forced to shut down and furlough its 80 employees. The 

SBA studied the firm's capabilities, found that it could produce component parts needed by a large company, and brought the two together. 

The small firm obtained an order and now has become one of the large concern's regular suppliers. 

SBA Intensifies

Subcontracting Efforts 

Late in 1961, Congress passed Public Law 87305 which established as Government policy that small business concerns "be considered fairly as subcontractors and suppliers to . . . prime contractors or subcontractors. 

Last year, SBA, the Department of Defense, the General Services Administration, and major prime contractors made significant progress in developing regulations and programs to increase subcontracting opportunities for small business. 

The SBA held planning meetings with the contracting agencies and approximately 20 of the largest prime contractors. These contractors were awarded more than half of the Government's contract awards in 1961, and have approximately 100 plants engaged in developing prototypes or building major assemblies, subassemblies, or component parts, for aircraft, ships, guided missiles, and space vehicles. 

Under cooperative arrangements, SBA will assist these prime contractors in finding small suppliers of items they have heretofore purchased exclusively or primarily from large firms. 

Obviously, the success of this program depends on close cooperation between the Government and prime contractors. The SBA therefore devoted much time last year to laying the basis for effec tive and coordinated action with purchasing agency representatives and prime contractors.

The preparatory work should bring increasing benefits to small firms in the months ahead. 

In the subcontracting program, SBA representatives will work closely with representatives of contracting agencies who are assigned to prime contractors' plants. The initial goal will be to locate additional small businesses for needed subcontracting work.

In accordance with section 8(d) (3) of the Small Business Act, a more detailed report on the subcontracting program is presented as appendix 

B of this report. 40
