SBA Certifies to Small Firms' Ability To Perform Contracts The SBA's Certificate of Competency (COC) 

Program helped a record number of small companies in 1962. The program provides, in effect, an appeals procedure for the small concern that bids low on a contract, but whose bid is rejected for reasons of inadequate productive capacity or credit. If the small firm appeals to SBA, the agency makes an independent study of its capabilities. If SBA certifies that the small firm can carry out the contract, the certification is binding on the contracting agency.

This SBA service has enabled many small companies to obtain Government contracts which otherwise would have gone to higher bidders.

In 1962, SBA issued 278 COC's relating to contracts totaling nearly $64 million; in 1961, it issued 

234 COC's relating to contracts totaling about $77 million. (Contract awards in 1962 resulting from COC's are listed in appendix A.) 

The 1,282 certificates of competency issued since 

1953 have resulted in 1,168 contract awards for approximately $231.7 million. Based on the difference between the small firms' bids and those of the next lowest bidders, the COC's have brought the Nation's taxpayers a saving of some $20.2 million.

In a typical 1962 case, SBA certified to the ability of a southwestern firm to manufacture 

200,000 steel water cans for the armed services.

The company had virtually completed production by the end of the year. Prior to issuance of the 

COC the firm employed 10 people; it now employs 

55. The increased employment is important to the firm's community since the closing of mines made it imperative to develop industrial payrolls. 

Another small concern, located in an area of substantial unemployment in Maine, bid on a military purchase of parts for tow targets. However, the contracting office questioned the firm's knowledge of the required manufacturing techniques. The SBA studied the firm's capabilities, then issued a COC. The company completed this $228,000 contract on schedule and since has ac quired other Government contracts. They have meant employment for more than 150 people.

Often, contracts are awarded to firms while their applications for COC's are still pending, because SBA already has developed sufficient favorable information to convince the contracting officer that the small firm can complete the contract successfully. An interior view of a busy plant in Chicago. Recipient of an SBA Certificate of Competency, this com pany went on to produce a complicated and delicate system of telephone communication equipment for the Army Signal Corps. The item, now being produced ahead of schedule, is of the highest quality. 42

For example, an Arkansas firm was rejected as he low bidder on a contract for wooden stepadders. The case was referred to SBA for a ossible COC. Favorable information developed n the SBA field survey was telephoned to the gency's representative at the purchasing office. 'he result was award of the contract to the small usiness.

Sole-Source" Purchases Opened to Small Firms 

During 1962 SBA helped to develop small usiness competition on many purchases which previously had been made on a "sole-source" basis. 

Frequently, competition for the contracts brought ower prices for the Government. 

For example, on a purchase of missile shipping torage containers, SBA found that the usual prime contractor regularly bought the containers from a small firm. The agency located other qualified small firms that were interested in biding, and the buying installation agreed to retrict the entire purchase to small business. As a esult four firms bid and a contract was awarded for $246,000-a lower price than that previously paid by the Government.

In another case, an SBA representative reviewed a $14 million project involving construction of a hurricane barrier in a harbor. Small businesses were not able to do the main part of the work the dredging and building of gates in the ship channel-but could construct a needed pumping station. This part of the project, with an estimated cost of $400,000, was set aside for small business. 

Small Firms May Pool Facilities 

The SBA may approve "pooling" of small business facilities for purposes of defense production, research and development, and acquisition of raw materials and equipment. Pools thus approved are exempt from the provisions of the antitrust laws and the Federal Trade Commission Act. 

In 1962, SBA approved one pool, the Federal 

Research and Development Corp. of New Orleans. 

It is a combination production-research and development pool made up of four companies with 277 employees. HACK Businessmen attending this SBA cosponsored procurement conference in a Midwestern city were among the early one million business owners and managers who attended 228 counseling conferences, meetings or rade conferences sponsored or participated in by SBA last year. 43

Seven pools discontinued operations during the year. Four were production pools and three were combination production-research and development pools. None had obtained contracts through the pool arrangement. 

As 1962 ended, 8 pools involving 61 companies with 3,644 employees were in operation.

SBA Business Counseling 

Conference Program

Another way in which SBA guides and assists small business is by cosponsoring business counseling conferences with State and local business groups, local officials and chambers of commerce. 

These conferences inform businessmen of SBA's contracts assistance and other services. 

The SBA also participates with other Government agencies, prime contractors, manufacturers, trade associations, and educational groups in broader conferences designed to help businessmen strengthen their domestic operations or develop and expand markets abroad. 

At these counseling conferences, information on selling to the Government and to prime contractors, as well as help with financial, technical and management problems, is provided by specialists from SBA and other Government agencies. Frequently, there are exhibits of items for which the Government or prime contractors need additional suppliers. 

Last year, SBA sponsored or participated in 

228 business counseling conferences, meetings and trade conferences throughout the country. These were attended by nearly 1 million business owners and managers. 

SBA Helps With 

Specifications Problems

Small concerns often have difficulty bidding on Government purchases because they are unable to obtain or review copies of applicable specifications. The SBA publication, "U.S. Government 

Purchasing, Specifications and Sales Directory," helps here by listing the locations of reference files of specifications and indexes, and giving other information. 

Another SBA service is study of proposed or existing Government specifications to determine whether they are proprietary and therefore prevent bidding by small concerns. Where this is true, SBA presses for elimination of the restrictive requirements.

An unusual specifications problem arose recently when manufacturers of electrical fuses included on the Government's qualified products list were notified that the list was to be canceled, and that each firm could regain listing only by requalifying its product at its own expense. This would have worked a financial hardship on 7 of the 10 listed firms, which are small businesses and do not have their own testing laboratories. When SBA protested to the purchasing agency that the ruling would subject the small concerns to prohibitive expense, it agreed to have the tests performed in 

Government laboratories.

Facilities Inventory Lists Productive Facilities 

Each SBA regional office maintains a register of small firms in its area. The inventory includes five types of productive facilities: industrial production; research and development; forest products; construction-contractor; and contractor services. The register of industrial production facilities is by far the largest part of the inventory. Information on small firms in this register is kept in the regions and on punch cards at SBA's Washington office. A register of small firms interested in foreign trade opportunities also is maintained in Washington.

The facilities inventory is widely used in sup port of SBA's contracts assistance and other programs. It is useful also to other Federal agencies, as well as to State and municipal govern ments, in programs of economic development. In time of national emergency, the information it provides would be vital to defense mobilization and postattack planning.

At the close of 1962, the facilities inventory included data on about 41,000 small firms. The ultimate goal is to list approximately 136,000 firms.

Other Federal agencies that use the facilities inventory are helping to expand it. The General 

Services Administration, Veterans Administra tion, Atomic Energy Commission, National Aeronautics and Space Administration, and the Department of Defense are distributing facilities inventory registration forms through their field offices.

The facilities inventory enables SBA to notify listed small concerns of impending Government contracts or subcontracts suitable to their facilities, as well as of foreign trade opportunities. 44

Conversely, it is an important source of small usinesses which have capabilities needed by Govrnment purchasing offices. 

Here are examples of how the facilities invenpry is used to assist small business:

Through the inventory, SBA is able to identify mall firms that may be interested in exhibiting heir products at foreign trade fairs. In one ase, SBA contacted a small midwestern manuacturer of firetrucks and told it of an opportunity o participate in an industrial exhibit at Damasus, Syria. As a result of participation, the company obtained a contract to provide firetrucks for Damascus, and established contacts with various nanufacturers' representatives. Since then, it as sold firefighting equipment to other foreign countries.

The Department of Defense asked SBA's help n locating small companies in areas of substantial nemployment that would want to bid on conracts for selected military items. In the last 2 nonths of 1962, SBA provided the names of 253 mall businesses that would be interested in bidding on various purchases. These companies are now on the bidders lists of the defense agencies and will receive invitations for bids on future requirements.

Small Firms Helped With Production, Other Problems 

Many small concerns do not have technical employees with the specialized experience needed to solve unusual production problems. Congress therefore provided in the Small Business Act that SBA counsel small firms on production difficulties. 

The agency gives such assistance through production and industrial specialists in its field offices. 

However, when problems are of broad scope SBA recommends that the firms use private consulting services.

A recent case illustrates the value of SBA technical assistance to small business. 

At the request of a midwestern hardboard manufacturer, the agency sought ways to improve the company's profit margin. An SBA production specialist recommended changes in plant layout and a switch from a two-shift to a threeshift operation to eliminate startup delays and quality problems which result from equipment cooling off during "down time." The SBA recommendations enabled the company to use faster and cheaper production methods. The re- 681420 0-63- sulting improvements in operating efficiency placed the company in a far stronger competitive position.

Small Business Share of Government Purchasing

Fiscal year 1962 saw a reversal of the downward trend in award of Government prime contracts to small business. Contract awards to small firms made up 19.3 percent of the total dollar value of awards the highest percentage of any fiscal year since 1957.

On a dollar basis, contracts awarded to small business in fiscal 1962 totaled $6.1 billion. Net awards to all business firms amounted to $31.5 billion, while overall Government purchases totaled $35.4 billion.

In fiscal year 1961, small businesses were awarded $4.9 billion of contracts, or 17.6 percent of the $27.6 billion of prime contract awards to business firms.

Of the total value of military awards in fiscal 1962, small business received approximately 17.7 percent, compared with 15.9 percent in fiscal 1961. 

The dollar amount of military awards to small firms in those years was approximately $4.6 and $3.7 billion, respectively.

Military Research and Development Awards. 

In fiscal year 1962, awards for experimental, developmental, test, and research work made up 

22.9 percent of the net value of military prime contracts for performance in the United States. 

In fiscal year 1961, contracts of this kind made up 

25.7 percent of such awards.

The small business share of the contracts during fiscal year 1962 was $197 million, or 3.5 percent of the awards to business firms totaling $5.7 billion. In fiscal year 1961, small firms received awards amounting to $161 million, or 2.9 percent of the contracts to business firms totaling $5.6 billion.

SBA Helps Small Firms Buy From the Government 

An important SBA responsibility is to help small firms purchase a fair share of property being sold by the Government. 

Under SBA agreements with other Federal agencies, Government sales frequently are earmarked for exclusive offering to small firms. 

These include sales of such property as "Liberty ships," material from the national stockpile, and timber. As another part of this SBA service, the agency notifies small concerns of forthcoming 

Government sales of machine tools, trucks, construction equipment, etc.

During 1962, the cooperative set-aside program resulted in the sale to small firms of four Liberty ships for scrapping purposes. In another instance, SBA and the General Services Adminis tration jointly set aside for small business a sale of 

4.8 million ounces of quinine. 

Timber Sales Assistance. In the field of forest products, SBA has small business set-aside agree ments with the Forest Service of the Department of Agriculture, the Bureau of Land Management 

(BLM) of the Department of the Interior, and the Department of Defense. These agreements provide for set-asides of timber sales for small sawmills or independent loggers unable to purchase Federal stumpage in open competition. In 

1962 SBA and cooperating agencies jointly set aside 96 timber sales with an estimated value of $3.3 million.

Forest Access Roads. During the year SBA 

and the Bureau of Land Management agreed to extend SBA's access-road loan program to sales of timber from forest lands under the jurisdiction of BLM. Under this program, previously appli cable only to sales conducted by the Forest Serv ice, SBA makes loans to small firms for the P construction of primary access roads when such roads are required by the timber sales contracts. 

Since the uncut timber serves as collateral for 8 loan, the program enables small businesses to com pete with large firms where sales require construct tion of roads before timber can be logged and, sold. During 1962, SBA approved 36 loans. totaling $2.3 million under the access-road loan program. 

Surplus Personal Property. The SBA helps small firms locate property being sold by the Fed eral Government for which the firms have specific need. By the close of 1962, more than 

16,000 had registered their needs with SBA. 

During the year some 5,200 registered small firms purchased about 15,000 Government items for $10.5 million. This property cost them an average of 20 to 30 percent of the original price, which represented a saving to the buyer and a good return to the Government. 48

Section VIII Encouraging Profitable Management 

When small business concerns fail and the jobs ney provide come to an end, the American econmy suffers. It suffers also when small firms do ot reach their full potential for growth. Busiess experts such as Dun & Bradstreet cite inadeuate management as the main reason for much f this loss to the national economy. 

In 1962, the Small Business Administration inreased its efforts to help small firms overcome management deficiencies and achieve stronger, nore profitable operations. The agency appointed 

Deputy Administrator for Management and Reearch Assistance; began an "Intraindustry Mangement Program," and broadened its existing rograms of counseling, management courses, con- Ferences, publications, and research. ntraindustry Management

Aims at Existing Channels 

Two events marked the start of SBA's Intra ndustry Management Program-publication of handbook designed to help large firms guide their mall customers and suppliers toward improved nanagement, and creation of an SBA task force o encourage and help industry to work with small irms. 

The new SBA handbook, "Suggested Managenent Guides," outlines the administrative managenent techniques necessary for profitable small usiness operations, and is valuable in developing >rograms to meet small business needs in specific ndustries. It is on sale by the Government Printng Office at $1.75.

During late 1962, SBA's intraindustry managenent task force worked closely with selected manuacturers, wholesalers, and trade associations to help them develop programs of management uidance for their small customers, suppliers, or nembers. Emphasis was placed on helping small irms do a better job in such areas as record keeping, market analysis, sales planning, personnel dministration, budgeting, and cost control. 

More Individuals Use SBA Counseling Service 

In 1962, SBA counseled more than 16,000 established or prospective small businessmen on management matters or problems, an increase of almost one-third over 1961. Inquiries received by the agency's Washington and field offices ranged from starting a new business, to selling handicrafted products, to details of operating ratios. 

The SBA issued 11 new "Small Business Bibli ographies" for counseling use, on such subjects as "Small Office Automation," "Consumer. Credit," "Catering," "Motels," and "Discount Retailing."

Because most of these leaflets deal with reference materials rather than news, the name "Small 

Business Bulletin," assigned to them earlier, was dropped in favor of "Small Business 

Bibliographies." 

In a major revision, material in two older publications, "Selling by Mail With Limited Capital" and "Mail Order Selling," was brought up to date and combined in a comprehensive bibliography, "Selling by Mail Order.”

The SBA also issues a series of "Counseling 

Notes," which are usually brief outlines of the fundamentals of starting particular businesses. 

New counseling notes issued in 1962 were "Films for Business," "Clipping Service," and "Editorial 

Service." Twelve notes were revised.

Both the small business bibliographies and the counseling notes support SBA's Management.

Counseling Program. Practical tools, responsive to individual inquiries, they save time for SBA's counselors, librarians, and other SBA personnel.

New records were set last year in the small business management course program. Courses are cosponsored by SBA and leading educational institutions. During the year, 501 courses were 49

One Small Firm's Experience. The experience f a small businessman who took an administrave management course illustrates the value of nis SBA service. 

Five years before, the businessman had bor Owed $15,000 from SBA, to use for working capal and other purposes. A year later, he owed 

BA $15,104 including interest, and his firm owed 

2,891 in Federal taxes.

Nearly broke, he attended an SBA administraive management course. By last spring, he had epaid his entire SBA loan, paid his back and urrent Federal taxes, and paid more than $6,000 f other debts. He was planning to expand. 

Asked if he saw a connection between the mangement course and the improvement of his busiess, he said: "That course was entirely and solely responsible or saving my business. Through it, I learned that management involves, and was able to idenify and correct ... my problems."

Program Stresses Continuing Education. 

Over one-fourth of the courses offered in 1962 rere advanced or followup courses, giving the mall businessmen-enrollees an opportunity to tudy thoroughly one or more subjects that were overed quickly in the basic course. Growth of he advanced courses indicates that both educaional institutions and small business owners recgnize the importance of continuing management ducation. Advanced courses in export trade and

J.S. Government contract administration were offered for the first time. 

Television as a Course Medium. More than 

900 businessmen registered for televised courses, with additional hundreds as viewers of those courses which were publicly broadcast. 

A midwestern course was televised by the De partment of Business Education, Public School 

System, of Des Moines, Iowa, and consisted of 10 sessions of 30 minutes each. An eastern course, given at Hagerstown, Md., in 1961, was reproduced on 16mm sound film in 1962 and was widely used. For example, WUNC-TV in Raleigh, N.C., broadcast the course twice, first on open circuit, then to 15 organized viewing groups on closed circuit. South Carolina's statewide education network presented the program to 13 different groups. 

Other users of the Hagerstown course included educational institutions in Alabama, Arizona, District of Columbia, Illinois, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, New York, Oklahoma, Texas, Virginia, West Virginia, and Wisconsin. 

Last spring, 27 participants in the Hagerstown pilot TV course were asked: "Would you prefer your next course over TV or with a classroom instructor?" Twenty-six said they preferred TV. 

Some typical comments: "I enjoyed TV as much or more than I would have enjoyed a live course." "We got a more concentrated and effective presentation over TV." "TV is an excellent way to instruct." 

Agency Cosponsors Many 

Management Conferences

In 1962, SBA cosponsored 132 management conferences attended by more than 15,000 small businessmen in 40 States. Three types of programs evoked special interest.

Forecasting Seminars. The SBA cosponsored two seminars in California, one with San Diego

State College, the other with the University of 

California, Los Angeles. These meetings featured discussions led by outstanding local businessmen and economists, and met with such excellent reception that SBA will schedule similar meetings in 1963.

Export Trade Management Conferences. Six ty-three were held in 29 States with 4,800 businessmen attending. Through these conferences, begun in 1961, SBA seeks to help attain the goals of the 

President's trade expansion program.

Tax Management Conferences. The SBA and the Internal Revenue Service held 22 of these in 51

States. More than 3,600 small business owners and managers, accountants, and lawyers attended. The conferences were a response to a recommendation of the White House Committee on Small Business, which found that small firms confronted with the complexities of Federal and State tax laws were often unable to take advantage of options and exemptions. 

Pilot conferences held at Syracuse, N.Y., and 

Peoria, Ill., in May enabled SBA and IRS to develop joint procedures.

The general response to the tax conferences was reflected in a program coordinator's remarks: "We received many favorable comments and the project goes into the book as our best effort of the year .. 99 

Briefing Foreign Visitors on SBA's Management Programs 

Government officials and educators from four continents were briefed during the year on SBA programs of administrative management development for small business. Such widely separated countries as Australia, Japan, Yugoslavia, Kenya, and Libya were represented, evidencing increasing world awareness of the importance of management education for owners of small firms. 

Orientation Program Developed For College Business Students 

On invitation of the Society for the Advancement of Management, SBA developed an orientation program on small business to be used in the society's university chapters. The program consisted of eight sessions which may be given individually or as a series. It calls for faculty advisers and student officers to brief chapter members on the importance of small business to the national economy. The SBA developed the session outlines and bibliographies on such subjects as "Small Business-Its Place in the Economy" and "Employment Opportunities in Small Business."

Multiplier Effect Increases Usefulness of Publications 

SBA management publications and the practical information they present reached more businessmen than ever during 1962. In part this was due to the fact that SBA encourages small businessmen and those who advise them to acquaint other potential users with the SBA publications or information, thus achieving a multiplier effect.

SBA-Financed Research

Helps Small Business 

Small business has continued to benefit from SBA-financed research into management problems peculiar to smaller concerns. The program has been conducted through grants authorized by the Small Business Act and through contracts let by SBA.

Grants Research. During the year, results of 

57 studies financed by grants were approved for publication. No grants have been made since June 

30, 1961. A total of 105 grants were awarded durng the fiscal years 1959 and 1960 and, when, all work resulting from these has been completed, the findings will be presented in about 250 reports. 

In 1962, SBA intensified its efforts to bring the results of research to the small business community as soon as possible, first by encouraging grantee institutions to speed up the completion of reports, and, secondly, by increasing the output of highly condensed summaries (33 of these 2page leaflets were issued during the year). 

There is growing evidence that the small business community finds the research studies and summaries valuable, and that they are circulated widely. They are used by individual business firms, by business consultants, by chambers of commerce, by trade associations, by Government agencies, and by members of Congress interested in small business legislation. 

As one indication of circulation, the mailing list for the summaries, started late in 1961 with a few hundred names, had grown by the close of 1962 to about 21,000. New names are being added at the rate of 500 monthly. Both summaries and reports are widely used also by the press. 

Of the 79 reports issued to date, the following have been best sellers: "A Study of Industry Financial and Operating Ratios," "Facts About 

Small Business Financing," "The Squeeze-Out in Small Business Ownership," "How Urban Re newal Projects Affect Small Business," and "In surance Management in Small Firms." 

For a list of the reports approved by SBA in 

1962, see appendix C.

A west-coast trade association requested several summaries for the "valuable information" they contained. The secretary of a chamber of commerce asked for multiple copies of "Use of Outside Information in Small Firms," for enclosure in his bulletin to chamber members. 

An elected official in a New England State, commenting on a study of how to stimulate greater development of natural resources, said it would be "particularly helpful" to his State's winter facilities development committee. A Congressman told SBA that the findings of a study of a depressed industry would be helpful to him in relation to legislation he was drafting. 

Contract Research. The SBA also obtains small business management research through contracts. In 1962, for example, it let a contract to determine the feasibility of using Internal Revenue Service data for compiling ratios helpful to small business. One research study, completed during the year by the University of Texas, is "A

Survey of University Business and Economic Re search Projects, 1959-63." It lists business research completed or in progress during those years at 280 universities and colleges in all parts of the 

United States. 55

Section IX Small Business Participation in Foreign Trade 

Small business concerns are becoming increasingly aware that, like the Nation itself, they have a vital stake in the future of international trade. 

Markets abroad are rich and growing, and represent a potential outlet that small concerns cannot neglect.

A growing number of progressive small businesses are now seeking entry to foreign markets. 

This is evidenced by the favorable responses SBA has received from small firms in soliciting and encouraging their participation in U.S. trade missions, international trade center shows, and trade fairs.

Several factors have contributed to the increased interest of small businesses in the foreign trade field. These include: The balance-of-payments deficit, which has focused attention on the need for increased exports; the National Export 

Expansion Program, which spurs export activities through trade missions, trade centers, and trade fairs; and creation of the Foreign Credit Insurance Association, through which American exporters can offer credit terms comparable to those of foreign competitors.

SBA Cooperating Closely 

With Commerce Department

While the trade missions, trade centers, and trade fairs programs are primarily the responsibility of the Department of Commerce, SBA cooperates closely with that Department by directly soliciting small business participation in the programs. In this way, SBA helps small firms promote the sale of their products abroad and make valuable contacts with foreign buyers and agents. 

Trade Centers. U.S. trade centers are permanent installations where a variety of industrial products are displayed. Each center has a trade promotion staff to attract buyers and prospective agents for the exhibited products. Only goods made in the United States can be displayed. 

U.S. trade centers are operating in London, Bangkok, and Frankfurt. Another center will open in Tokyo in early 1963.

By arrangement with the Department of Commerce, SBA invited small manufacturers to exhibit their products at the following trade center shows: 

Toy and game show-London; automotive service and maintenance equipment show-Frankfurt; small farm machinery and irrigation equipment show-Bangkok; industrial instruments and laboratory apparatus show-Tokyo; toy and game show-Frankfurt; business machines and office equipment show-Bangkok; and the automotive service and maintenance equipment show-Tokyo 

Trade Center. 

On the average, between 30 and 60 exhibitors are able to display their products at a single show. 

Numerous small concerns are participating in these shows as exhibitors, and at some of the larger shows fully half of the exhibitors have been small manufacturers. 

Trade Missions. A trade mission is made up of selected business specialists who carry specific business proposals to international markets as representatives of the U.S. business community. The mission spends from 3 to 5 weeks in a specific country, meeting with individual businessmen, business groups, the press, public officials, and trade associations to help establish trade relationships between U.S. firms and their oversea counterparts. Upon their return, mission members meet in "end-of-mission" conferences with businessmen at various cities throughout the country, to discuss trade and investment opportunities the mission has developed. 

The SBA encourages small businessmen to participate in the trade missions program by serving as mission members, by submitting business pro- 56

posals, and by attending the end-of-mission conferences to obtain firsthand information about trade and investment opportunities. Almost half of the mission members are small businessmen. 

SBA field offices advise small firms of forthcoming trade missions and urge that they prepare business proposals to be carried abroad by the missions. During the past year SBA solicited business proposals from small businessmen for trade missions to the United Kingdom, Germany, Israel, and Cyprus. SBA's Deputy Administrator for Procurement and Technical Assistance served as director of the mission to the last two countries. 

The SBA also participated with the Department of Commerce in 13 meetings throughout the

United States, where small business owners conferred with mission members on oversea business ■ opportunities.

Trade Fairs. The purpose of U.S. participa tion in trade fairs is to tell the story of American free enterprise and to sell U.S. goods. Small business concerns are urged by SBA to exhibit their products in U.S. exhibitions at international = trade fairs. Greater small business participation is anticipated during 1963 because an augmented international trade fair program has been planned. 

Individual Counseling Provided On Foreign Trade Operations 

Small businesses which contemplate establishing or expanding export operations are counseled by SBA on the principal points to be considered. 

The basic points include a company's financial situation, the availability and competency of management personnel to plan and carry out an export program and other management details. 

In addition, SBA furnishes leads to sources which can help meet the specific needs of the small I concern, and serves as liaison between Government and private industry sources and the small business. Where necessary information is not readily available, SBA obtains the information for the business. Referrals are made to appro- -priate sources when the small concern requires technical information on such subjects as tariffs, 1 documentation, controls, and packaging.

Foreign Trade Research Helps Small Firms 

The SBA has financed special studies on foreign trade, to help small firms interested in this field. 

An export marketing primer titled "Export

Marketing for Smaller Firms" has been prepared for SBA by the International Marketing Institute of the Harvard Graduate School of Business Ad ministration.

It will be available from the 

Superintendent of Documents, U.S. Government 

Printing Office, about May 1963. The publication discusses factors to consider in deciding whether to export, outlines a step-by-step procedure to follow when entering foreign markets for the first time, and gives sources of information on foreign trade. 

Another study, completed in 1962 by the St. 

Louis University under an SBA grant, is titled 

"The Participation of Missouri Firms in Export 

Trade." A similar study by Lehigh University on the subject "Building Small Business Profits

Through Exports to Latin America" will be published in 1963. 

Conferences and Courses 

Cosponsored by SBA

As part of its regular conferences and courses program, SBA has cosponsored 63 conferences to acquaint small businessmen with export trade operations. These conferences are cosponsored with local chambers of commerce, other trade groups, and educational institutions. The SBA also has increased the number of courses it cosponsors with educational institutions to provide detailed information on foreign trade subjects. 

These courses are given one night a week over an 

8to 10-week period.

Liaison Developed With Other 

Agencies and Groups

One of SBA's principal liaison activities is assuring adequate consideration of the needs of small business in the programs of other Government agencies. In the field of international trade the 

Department of Commerce has primary responsibilities through its Bureau of International Commerce. The SBA maintains a close working relationship with the Department, both in Washington and in the field. Corresponding relationships have been established with the Agency for 

International Development and the Export- 

Import Bank.

In the area of private industry, SBA has established liaison with many groups that are endeavoring to expand export trade, including the Chamber of Commerce of the United States, local chambers of commerce, trade associations, and commercial banks which have international departments. 57

Trade Expansion Act To Benefit Small Firms 

Impetus was given the National Export Expan sion Program by the enactment in October 1962 

of the Trade Expansion Act, legislation which should stimulate and enlarge small business participation in export markets.

As to small manufacturers in particular, the act affords a twofold benefit. On the one hand, small producers engaged in or capable of entering export markets, stand to gain from trade concessions obtained by U.S. negotiators from other nations. 

On the other hand, manufacturers, including small ones, whose production or marketing operations require adjustment to increased foreign competition resulting from reciprocal tariff concessions granted by the United States may qualify for adjustment assistance.

Following a determination by the Tariff Commission that a firm has been injured by increased imports and approval by the Secretary of Commerce of the firm's proposal for its economic adjustment, the firm becomes eligible to apply for long-term loans, tax relief, and technical assistance in furtherance of the adjustment. Public Law 

87-550, enacted in 1962, empowers SBA to make the loans.

In establishing an Adjustment Assistance Ad visory Board to advise the President on and to coordinate the assistance program, the act recognized the special stake of small business in the program by designating the Administrator of the ze SBA as the only non-cabinet member of the Board. 3 58

Section X Improving SBA Operations and Administration A principal goal of the Small Business Admin istration in 1962 was to give greater assistance to small business while at the same time achieving increased efficiency in operations-in short, to provide maximum service at minimum cost. 

Toward this end, SBA made comprehensive studies of operating procedures under its small business assistance programs, and of personnel, training, and other aspects of agency administration. These studies resulted in better and quicker service for small business, increased SBA productivity, and a reduction in agency expenditures. 

The SBA's services to small business were further strengthened by adoption of recommendations formulated during the year by the SBA's national, regional, and State small business advisory councils. 

Surveys Provide Basis

For Agency Improvements 

During the year, the agency surveyed the effectiveness of its small business assistance programs in 33 field offices, and evaluated management efficiency in 12 offices and use of personnel in 8 others.

These studies led to better service to small firms, such as more effective help in obtaining surplus Government property, and to numerous management economies and improvements. For example, they resulted in better use of personnel, property, and space; to increased delegations of authority to field offices; and to improved accounting and loan operations. 

Providing Better, Quicker

Service for Small Business 

One means by which SBA achieves better use of personnel, and at the same time gives improved service to small firms, is by delegating authority to its field offices wherever feasible. 

To illustrate, during 1962 the agency developed a plan under which regional directors would be authorized to issue certificates of competency (discussed in sec. VII) where the Government contracts involved do not exceed $50,000. This plan was field tested in the New York regional office. 

The test proved the value of the plan, and the decision was made to extend similar approval authority to all regional offices in 1963. 

A survey of SBA assistance to small firms in obtaining surplus Government property indicated a need for complete reorganization of the program. Under the reorganization, to be completed early in 1963, SBA employees engaged in this work will be assigned to SBA offices nearest the Defense Supply Agency field offices. This will result in better coordination of the two agencies' work, and make it possible to reduce from 14 to 7 the number of SBA employees assigned to property sales assistance. 

Training of Employees 

Intensified in 1962

The SBA's productivity and efficiency were increased last year through intensified training of 

Washington and field personnel. To illustrate, under one training program, supervisory personnel of the agency were instructed in management responsibilities. In another program, young college graduates who majored in economics, business administration, accounting or finance were trained for positions in SBA's lending operations. 

The SBA gave special emphasis during the year to training personnel to function in a national emergency. Washington officials conducted training sessions at all regional offices to assure field office ability to perform under emergency conditions. In addition, plans and procedures for emergency use were reviewed, and, where necessary, revised. 59

Contributing also to increased efficiency of personnel in 1962 was a program for shortening and simplifying SBA interoffice communications and instructions. As part of this program, employees were given extensive training in the writing of procedures, and new manuals which incorporate the best known practices in writing procedures and instructions were developed. This program has helped speed up operations and increase productivity.

Electronic Processing Expanded To Additional SBA Operations 

A committee established early in the year to review the efficiency of SBA's automatic data processing system found that on the accounting operations for which it was installed it provided faster, more accurate accounting and saved manpower. 

At the committee's recommendation, the system was extended to payroll preparation, to the financial reporting required of small business investment companies, and to maintenance of the "facilities inventory," through which SBA informs purchasing agencies of the capabilities of small firms interested in obtaining Government contracts.

These extensions of the automatic data processing system have added to efficiency. For example, use of the system for the facilities inventory makes it possible to maintain listings on a current and accurate basis, and to provide information on productive capacity more rapidly.

New Loan Plan 

Lessens Workload

In September 1962, SBA introduced a new 

Early Maturity Loan Plan which complements the Simplified Bank Participation Plan begun in 1961. In the new plan, as in the earlier one, SBA relies greatly on the recommendations of banks in processing loan applications. In both plans, the application forms and other papers are drastically reduced and simplified, lessening the requirements on applicants and the workload on SBA and the banks. 

Streamlining, Training 

Bring Budgetary Savings 

By streamlining procedures, and by additional training of personnel, SBA has been able to increase the workload standards for employees assigned to loan processing and loan administration. 

For processing of regular SBA business loans, the workload standard has been increased from 6 to 

612 per man-month; for processing applications under the Limited Loan Participation Plan and

Simplified Early Maturity Participation Plan, the standard has been increased from 12 to 14 per man-month; and in loan administration the stand-a ard for bank-serviced business loans has been increased from 125 to 150 cases for each technical employee. 

Because of the new standards, SBA's fiscal year 1963 budget request to Congress provided 56 fewer man-years for financial assistance than would have been required under the former standards. The additional cost under the former standards would have been $483,000.

A saving has been made also in regard to verifying payments of premiums on insurance policies of SBA borrowers who assigned the agency an interest in the policies. Under electronic accounting systems now used by most insurance companies, no receipts are issued for premium payments. This meant that SBA had to mail out thousands of inquiries yearly as to the status of premium payments. By arrangements concluded during the year, insurers now notify SBA automatically of premium defaults before cancellation of policies. This has ended the need for SBA mailings and saved 25 to 30 man-years of work. 

Advisory Councils Meet, Submit Recommendations to SBA

The SBA has completed the organization of its national, regional, and State small business advisory councils, and has enlarged the National Council so that each State, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico will have a member. At the yearend, the membership of all councils stood at about 1,000, representing a national cross section of the principal types of small business. 

The council members, predominantly owners and managers of small businesses (all serving without pay), evaluate existing and proposed SBA programs, recommend the undertaking of specific programs, comment on general small business problems, and promote the welfare of small business in furthering appropriate functions of SBA. 

In keeping with Federal regulations, SBA personnel serve as council chairmen.

The National Council met with the SBA Administrator in Washington, D.C., on two occasions

60

last year, May 7-8 and October 16-17. At the second meeting, it unanimously recommitted itself to "improving communications between the area each represents and the Small Business Administration, to the end that the entire scope of the facilities and services which the Administration is prepared to offer small business is fully and forcefully presented."

National Council members expressed their full and complete support of SBA's credit policies, and urged continued cooperation between SBA and commercial banking institutions.

Among recommendations adopted by the National Council were these: 

That SBA should maintain a stand in defense of the construction set-aside program. 

That the agency continue to work as diligently in the future as it has in the past in cooperative effort with commercial banks, specifically with regard to the Simplified Early Maturity Plan, and that this effort also be made through State banking associations.

That SBA continue and emphasize its program of the sale of seasoned loans to banks, making every effort to acquaint banks with the availability of these loans. 

That relations be established with chambers of commerce, trade associations, banks, local post offices, and other business organizations in cities without SBA field offices and, where practical, that a rack of free SBA descriptive leaflets and bulletins be supplied and kept up to date; that the name and address of the nearest SBA field office be at the top of the rack and if an SBA circuit rider serves the community through the local organization, that the facts as to his regular time and place of availability also be made known. 

That efforts be accelerated and intensified for providing procurement, technical, and foreign trade assistance to small business concerns, and that all field offices of the agency be adequately staffed so that these services can be made readily available and accessible. 

The advice of regional and State councils was drawn upon at approximately 70 meetings. The councils adopted several hundred recommendations, all of which were carefully considered and many implemented by SBA. 681420 0-63- 61

Section XI Defining "Small Business 

Under the Small Business Act and the Small 

Business Investment Act, the Small Business Ad ministration's primary responsibility is to assist those concerns which qualify as "small business."

The Small Business Act defines a small business in general terms, and authorizes SBA to adopt more specific definitions or size standards, and to determine within each industry which concerns are to be designated "small business."

As it gains experience in the operation of its programs, and as changes occur in the relative size of companies within industries, SBA makes necessary revisions in its basic size standards and in procedures for determining the size of specific concerns. During 1962, the agency made a number of such changes in its size standards and procedures. 

In addition, SBA is developing procedures for use in certifying concerns as eligible for priority payments under the War Claims Act of 1948, as amended. This new certification responsibility was assigned to the agency by an amendment 

(Public Law 87-846) to the War Claims Act of 

1948.

Elimination of SBA Certificate Program 

Perhaps the most significant change of the year was ending of the "Small Business Certificate 

Program" as of September 26, 1962. 

Prior to that date, a concern with no more than 

1,000 employees which failed to qualify as a small business for the purpose of Government contracting because its employment exceeded the general size standard of 500 employees could apply to SBA for a small business certificate. If SBA found that a limited number of concerns with 500 or fewer employees manufactured the product for which certification was requested, and that a few large concerns accounted for most of the production, it would certify that the applicant was a small business. 99 

The SBA used this certification procedure because the 500-employee size standard was too low in the case of certain industries, yet purchasing agencies were reluctant to depart from this traditional dividing line between large and small b business.

On the other hand, small business certificates often were criticized as favoring particular companies. Moreover, the Small Business Act re- de quires that when number of employees is used as a criterion, size standards shall vary from industry to industry. Although SBA had been adopting specific standards for various industries for some time, it decided that adoption of complete industry-by-industry standards would permit more concerns which meet the standards to certify themselves as small business without aplying to SBA for certification.

Consequently, the agency studied the relative size of concerns in each of the 426 manufacturing industries, and on the basis of these studies proposed adoption of a specific size standard of 500, 750, or 1,000 employees for each industry. The

SBA believes that self-certification under these multiple size standards will prove vastly superior to the Small Business Certificate Program. 

Agency Defines "Small Business" For Subcontracting Program 

The establishment of a Government Small Busi ness Subcontracting Program under Public Law 

87-305, enacted in September 1961, made it necessary for SBA to define small business for subcontracting purposes.

Originally, it was felt that the definitions used for Government prime contracting purposes should be used also for subcontracts. However,

SBA found that use of these definitions, without appropriate modifications, would present a difficult and expensive accounting problem for Government prime contractors, particularly in the case of small purchase orders placed with dealers and 62

distributors. Therefore, certain requirements were waived in the case of subcontracts not exceeding $2,500. 

Progress in Simplifying

Size Standards Regulation 

The SBA has made considerable progress in simplifying the Small Business Size Standards 

Regulation so that it may be used more readily by small businessmen and other Government agencies. 

Early in 1963, the agency will publish a Size De termination Handbook, a Procurement Size Stand ards Guide, and a new revision of the Small 

Business Size Standards Regulation. Each of these will help to simplify the size determination process.

Several Standards 

Revised or Adopted

During 1962, SBA adopted specific size stand- Yards for small business within several industries and revised existing standards in others. 

Financial Assistance. For purposes of its finan cial assistance program, the agency established specific standards for trailer courts and parks, hospitals, convalescent and nursing homes, medical and dental laboratories, and shopping center operators.

Based on new studies and experience, the agency increased its size standards for small manufacturers of ice cream, small arms, and small arms ammunition.

The definition of a small concern in the air transportation industry was changed to a basis of annual receipts rather than average employment. 

Government Contracting. The agency also revised several standards used for the purpose of 

Government contracting. The size criteria for small manufacturers of rubber footwear, tires and inner tubes, small arms and small arms ammunition, and of a small concern in the construction industry were increased to reflect changes in the relative size of concerns in those industries. 

Because of the extensive use of casual employees in the custodial and janitorial services industry, the definition of a small business in that industry was changed to a basis of average annual receipts rather than average number of employees. 

Several Changes Made in Size Standards Differentials 

Under certain circumstances involving special help to areas of substantial unemployment, SBA has permitted a concern to exceed the definition of a small business by 25 percent and still qualify as small business. During 1962 several changes were made in the rules governing such size differentials. 

For the purpose of Government contracting, the 25-percent differential applicable to a concern performing all or most of a particular contract in an area of substantial unemployment was eliminated.

For the purpose of SBA financial assistance, the regulation was amended so that the 25-percent differential now applies only if the concern in question will use the loan in an area that has been classified as an area of substantial unemployment continuously since September 1961. 

However, a 25-percent differential was made applicable to any concern which will use its loan in an area classified as a redevelopment area ur der the Area Redevelopment Act.

Investment Act, are financed from a revolving fund.

In authorizing such appropriations, the Congress has establishel maximum dollar amounts of loans and investments and commitments therefor which may be outstanding at any one time. Status of Revolving Fund 

No

The amount appropriated through December 31, 1962, as the capital investment of the Government in the revolving fund is $1,260 million. This capital investment, together with interest, fee, and miscellaneous income, and repayments on loans and debentures held, comprises the total available to cover the Small Business Administration's share of authorized loans and debenture purchases, administrative expenses incurred in making and servicing loans, and interest payments to the Treasury Department on the net amount expended from the fund.

Prior to fiscal year 1963, administrative expenses of the Investment and Development Company Assistance Program were not charged against the fund, since the Small Business Investment Act specifically provided that these expenses were to be financed by direct appropriations. Through 

June 30, 1962, these expenses totaled $4,817,262.9

The Small Business Investment Act Amendments of 1961 (Public Law 87-341) changed this requirement, and effective July 1, 1962, administrative expenses for the Investment and Development

Company Assistance Program, as well as the Financial Assistance Program, are being financed by a transfer from the revolving fund.

A detailed statement of the charges and credits to the fund through December 31, 1962, is shown in appendix D. As will be observed, the cumulative total of Government funds invested in loans and debentures amounted to $1,322 million and undisbursed commitments on December 31, 1962, amounted to almost $168 million. A total of $250 million from appropriations and receipts remained available for future use. These figures are exclusive of investments and commitments reported under the head of Reconstruction Finance Corpo ration Liquidation Program. 

Concerning this latter program, effective July 1, 1962, the remaining assets and liabilities of the 

Reconstruction Finance Corporation Liquidation 

Fund were transferred to the SBA revolving fund for liquidation purposes. This transfer had been approved as a part of the 1963 budgetary program 64

of SBA, and permitted the elimination of separate accounting records and budget schedules for a relatively nominal amount of assets remaining out of those transferred to the agency for administration and liquidation under the provisions of 

Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954 and Reorganization Plan No. 1 of 1957. A summary statement covering the progress in liquidation of these RFC assets is shown in appendix E. 

Operating Results of Programs 

As shown in the financial statements in appendix D, the agency's lending and investment operations over a period of about 9½ years have resulted in a cumulative deficit of $84,537,962, including a loss of $20,444,983 for calendar year = 1962.

An accompanying table reflects a distribution of the income and expense amounts for the year among the three program activities financed from the revolving fund. This distribution is based to the extent possible on accounting records, and on statistical records for the remainder. Comparative data is given for 1960 and 1961. 

In appraising the net loss produced by these program activities, several factors peculiar to SBA's operations as compared with those of a private financial institution must be taken into consideration. 

1. For example, by statute, the maximum interest rate on disaster loans is fixed at 3 percent, and the maximum interest rate on business loans is set at 512 percent. At the same time, in fiscal year 1963 SBA is paying the Treasury Department interest on outstanding disbursements from the fund at rates of 35%, 334, and 3% percent (depending on the average maturity of the outstanding loans). In fiscal year 1962, interest paid to the Treasury was based on a composite rate of 35% percent. 

2. Since SBA's primary purpose is to aid and assist small business, and lending is one of the tools available to the agency for carrying out this purpose, many applications are accepted and worked on even though they do not result in loans. This frequently involves as much personnel costs as though the loans were made. Also, since SBA does not have previous credit files on applicants and many of them are located in cities and towns remote from SBA offices, the investigation of applications often involves more time and cost than a private lending institution would be willing to expend. 

3. Further, the average size business loan is small, and the resulting income consequently is small in relation to the work involved in the processing, closing, and servicing functions. 

Loss Experience on 

Loans and Investments

It will be observed from the table showing distribution of income and expenses by program activities that the loss for each of the three activities results in part from the charge against earnings for a provision for estimated future losses on loans and investments made during the year. The establishment of reserves for losses through monthly charges to earnings at predetermined rates applied to the amount of loan and investment disbursements is predicated on the assumption that some loans and investments will prove to be uncollectible. In keeping with normal accounting practices, the potential loss should be recognized in the year of disbursement through the use of the reserve technique.

Because of the magnitude of the job involved, an analysis of actual and projected losses in relation to the rates used to establish reserves for such losses is made only once each year, as of June 30.

The results of the analyses made as of June 30, 1962, for business and disaster loans, are discussed in the paragraphs that follow. (Similar detailed analyses with respect to loans to small business investment companies and State and local development companies, and purchases of SBIC debentures, have not been made, since no losses have yet materialized. However, it should be noted that these particular loan programs have been in existence only since the fall of 1958.)

Business Loans. Through June 30, 1962, SBA 

had incurred a loss on 456 business loans, and had charged off a total of $5,647,545 of unpaid principal. Further, the field staff had estimated a loss on 575 additional loans in the amount of $8,243,000, for a total actual and estimated loss of $13,890,545. 

This represents 1.3 percent of loans disbursed through that date.

Appendix B = 

Report on the Government's Small Business Subcontracting Program¹ 

The Small Business Subcontracting Program was established by Public Law 87-305 (September 26, 1961), which amended the Small Business Act. 

The provisions of the new law were placed in effect through a program developed jointly by the 

Small Business Administration, the Department of Defense, and the General Services Adminis tration. Regulations implementing the program were issued in February 1962, and are now a part of the Armed Services Procurement Regulation 

(ASPR) and the Federal Procurement Regulations (FPR). Major provisions instruct military and civilian contracting personnel to include in future contracts over $500,000 clauses requiring prime contractors to establish small business subcontracting programs. Thus, the requirements of the new legislation are applied to prime contractors through provisions of their basic contracts.

Responsibility for determining a prime contractor's compliance with his contractual obligations in regard to subcontracting rests with the contracting Government agency. The SBA has participated with contracting agencies in a few reviews of contractor compliance. However, until the agency has gained more experience in this connection it will not be in a position to make an overall evaluation of the extent to which contractors are fulfilling their obligations.

Prime contractors' statistical reports on subcontracting are made directly to the contracting agencies.

In this regard, a report of the Defense Department dated February 8, 1963, shows a relative decrease in the ratio of payments to small business firms by the large defense contractors reporting under the Defense Subcontracting Program. In the period July 1 through December 31, 1962, 1 In accordance with sec. 8 (d) (8) of the Small Business Act. small firms received $1,943,600,000 in subcontracting payments, compared with $1,872,140,000 in the same period of 1961. Although this is an increase in dollars, when the small business portion is related to military prime contracting, the small business portion dropped from 15.7 to 15.3 percent. The report points out that total payments to all small firms by all prime contractors may be understated, since some large contractors are not required to report, and those who do report are required to provide information only with respect to contracts or subcontracts over $500,000. 

In contacting the 20 largest prime contractors (1962 list), SBA has observed that most prime contractors responded very quickly to the new requirements, voluntarily extending the applicability of the new provisions to existing contracts, even though some of these contracts were entered into prior to passage of the new law and were thus technically exempt. The SBA also has noted that most of the prime contractors it contacted were prompt to establish internal policies and procedures needed to carry out the intent of the program.

The SBA program activities in subcontracting are directed toward encouraging and assisting prime contractors to increase small business participation in their purchases. Under ASPR and 

FPR provisions, prime contractors are required to seek small firms capable of participating in certain contracts otherwise to be awarded to large firms.

They are required to do so, however, only when contracts are of such magnitude that the purchasing agency has required preprocurement approval.

It is believed that the statute is sufficiently broad to encompass smaller contracts, and progress is being made by SBA to enlist the voluntary 74

cooperation of prime contractors in applying the program to contracts not requiring purchasing agency approval. In fact, some firms have indicated their willingness to apply this voluntary approach to commercial production as well as to 

Government contracting. 

In respect to both the Government-wide progam and SBA's role, progress is understandably slow, as is likely in any new program which involves a number of participants, with differing functions and primary objectives. In industry, as in Government, having people pursue rapidly an objective which is secondary to their central mission is difficult at best. It is particularly so in relation to complex and costly weapons and spacecraft production, where capability is more frequently considered to be proportionate with corporate size. 

In enacting Public Law 87-305, Congress envisaged this new legislation as an instrument for getting a larger share of subcontract dollars into the hands of small firms. In calling for a reversal of the downward trend, it placed a mutual responsibility upon contracting agencies, industry participants, and the SBA to contribute jointly to the attainment of this vital economic objective. 75

III. Investment and Development Company Assistance and Supervision: Licensing, regulating, and examining small business investment companies, providing capital and lending funds to such companies; and lending funds to State and local development companies____. 

IV. Procurement Assistance: Assisting small firms to obtain a greater proportion of total purchases and contracts for supplies and services for the Government: 

A. Joint set-asides (assisting small business in obtaining contracts for supplies and services in connection with civilian agency requirements and for mobilization and defense, including research and development contracts)-- B. Certificates of competency (certifying to Government procurement officers with respect to the competency, as to capacity and credit, of any small business concern or group of such concerns to perform a specific Government contract)_.
