EXPLANATION OF STAFFING 

Program offices: Personnel assigned to these offices provide technical direction to the agency's field staff. Within each of the programs, staffs of specialists are dealing with specific areas of operation. In addition to promulgating program directives, the Washington staff reviews field operations through use of reporting systems and conducts site reviews through field visits.

There are certain instances in which the Washington staff has reserved the authority to act on program matters; e.g., approval of certificates of competency on contracts in excess of $50,000, approval of direct loans in excess of $50,000, approval of participation loans in excess of $150,000. Also, the licensing of small business investment companies and approval of loans to these companies are processed by the Washington staff in accordance with expressions of the Congress.

Administrative offices: Personnel assigned to these offices provide direction of and perform services in the usual areas of loan and administrative accounting, procurement and property supply management, personnel recruitment and administration, and fiscal operations concerned with the handling of loan payments and custody of loan collateral documents. While these activities are conducted = primarily on a centralized basis, certain of the fiscal operations and personnel actions are handled by field personnel under decentralized authority. In addition, this grouping includes the Office of Economic Adviser and Of fice of General Counsel, whose staffs provide advice and counsel and perform the duties associated with such organizational units. Field

SBA conducts its program operations through 64 field offices, and in addition, has personnel working in Federal procurement centers and in plants of large military prime contractors.

A majority of the field staff, i.e., personnel engaged in financial assistance activities, is assigned strictly on the basis of workload. Using budgetary standards that have been accepted by the Bureau of the Budget and the appropriations committees, personnel needs are determined and continuously reviewed by the application of these standards to workload. For program activities that do not lend themselves to a "work standard," personnel assignments are made consistent with resources provided to the agency and to programs and locations in such manner as to serve best the small business community. 

Workload data are contained in the respective program sections of this report. Detailed justifications of the agency and to programs and locations in such manner Congress each year and are contained in the published hearings of the House and Senate Appropriations Committees. 91

Appendix H 

General Accounting Office Reports and SBA Comments 

During 1962, the General Accounting Office issued two reports on Small Business Administration operations, one dealing with SBA's fee arrangements with lending institutions participating in small business loans, the other with the SBA-purchasing agencies' joint set-aside program. 

A summary of the GAO reports, as presented by that agency to the Committee on Appropriations, U.S. House of Representatives, and SBA comments on each report, follow:

GAO REPORT 

"Need for SBA To Reconsider Its Fee Arrangements with Lending Institutions "Under the Small Business Act, the Small Business Administration (SBA) is authorized to make loans to small-business establishments either directly or in participation with lending institutions, usually commercial banks. SBA participates with the banks in two types of loans, deferred-participation and immediate-participation. In the deferred-participation type of loan, SBA commits itself to purchase from a participating bank a percentage of the unpaid balance of a loan at such time as the bank may request. In the immediate-participation type of loan, SBA purchases a percentage of the loan from the participating bank at the time the loan is made. In each type the extent of SBA's participation, not to exceed 90 percent, is stipulated in the loan agreement. "In our report to the Congress dated April 25, 1962, on the fee arrangements made by SBA with lending institutions that participate in making business loans to small concerns, we commented on the effects of changes made by SBA in these fee arrangements effective on April 1, 1958.

Under the new fee arrangement, SBA began paying a service fee to banks servicing immediateparticipation business loans and reduced by 50 percent a fee which banks have to pay for SBA's commitment to purchase a percentage of the unpaid balance of deferred-participation business loans. The new fee arrangements have cost the agency about $1.8 million on new loans made during the first 3 years the new fees were in effect. "SBA has incurred an additional cost of $1.3 million as a result of its decision to voluntarily make the new fee arrangements applicable to loans outstanding at the time of the changes.

Further, since the increased cost of the fee changes is of a continuing nature, the new service fee and reduced participation fee will increase SBA's operating loss by an amount which we estimated will reach $1.1 million annually by fiscal year 1963. "SBA's objectives in revising its fee arrangements were to increase bank participation in SBA business loans and to reduce SBA's loan servicing costs. In our report, however, we expressed the belief that SBA realized but limited success in attaining these objectives as, under the new arrangements, the amount of deferred-participation loans approved-the type of loans having the highest priority under the Small Business Actdecreased and, as noted above, the agency incurred significant additional costs. "In October 1961 the Administrator, SBA, informed us that a drastic change in the fee structure was not warranted at that time. However, because of the continuing high cost of the new fee arrangements and other adverse effects, we recommended that the Administrator reconsider all phases of the agency's fee policy and make such changes as are appropriate."

92

SBA COMMENTS ON GAO REPORT 

(In form of a letter from the SBA Administrator to 

Senator Spessard L. Holland)

Hon. SPESSARD L. HOLLAND, 

U.S. Senate, Washington, D.C. 

MAY 11, 1962. 

DEAR SENATOR HOLLAND: This letter is in response to a request from a staff member of the 

Senate Appropriations Subcommittee for SBA views on the GAO report numbered B-114835, dated April 25, 1962, on "Review of Fee Arrangements With Lending Institutions Small Business Administration."

I view the independent appraisal by GAO of the small business program not only as a means of assurance to Congress that its policy intentions Fare being carried out, but also as a fresh viewpoint and means of further information to me regarding the effects of my policies on the program operations. However, this particular report is based on hypothesis as to what the results under the small business programs might have been had a I different combination of policy decisions been made. I believe that in this conjectural area, we need to look carefully at all sides of the problem. 

The policy decisions in question were made by a previous Loan Policy Board, consisting of the (then) Administrator of SBA, Secretary of Commerce, and Secretary of Treasury. With the advantage of hindsight, it appears possible that our present Loan Policy Board might have arrived at some different conclusions. However, after having reviewed the circumstances in existence at the time the decisions were made, I am of the opinion that the policies adopted appeared logical under the circumstances.

Evaluation of these policy decisions should be made in a context that emphasizes the over-all program objectives under which they were made. 

One of the primary program objectives with respect to the business loan program and as recognized in the legislation is that private credit shall be utilized if at all possible. For example, section 

7(a) of the Small Business Act provides, "No financial assistance shall be extended unless . the financial assistance applied for is not otherwise available on reasonable terms." Also". 

Also "... no loan be made unless it is shown that a particimay pation is not available," and "No immediate participation may be purchased unless it is shown that a deferred participation is not available.” 681420 0-63- -7 These principles recognize the desirability of private financing not only from the standpoint of reducing the drain on the Federal Treasury, but also in aiding the small business applicant to obtain a credit standing for his future benefit. 

As the table on page 24 of the GAO audit report reflects, there had been a gradual decrease in the proportion of participation loans both in terms of dollars as well as numbers, and during fiscal year 

1958, there was a sizable drop of 6 percent in terms of dollars and 8 percent in number of participation loans. In retrospect, considering this trend and the sharp change which occurred in 

1958, some remedial action appeared advisable.

The table in the report reveals that the downward trend was reversed in terms of dollars in the fiscal year 1959 and the decrease in terms of numbers of loans was more moderate in 1959 and was reversed in 1960. It would appear, therefore, that the action taken on April 1, 1958, accomplished the desired results.

As pointed out by the GAO report, it is true that within the participation loan category, the proportion of loans of the immediate participation type continued to increase, whereas the proportion of deferred participation loans has continued to decline. It is interesting to note, however, that in the fiscal year 1959, the percentage of total loans approved in the deferred participation category increased over the fiscal year 1958 which would indicate that the policy decision made on 

April 1, 1958, did have some impact.

It has been our observation that the most critical element in a bank's decision whether to make a loan on the immediate or the deferred participation basis is the question of available funds. As conceded by GAO on page 16 of the report in response to my position in this matter, if a bank has excess funds to lend, it would prefer to earn 

5.4 percent on the total dollars rather than 7.5 percent on only one-fourth of the amount, with the rest of the funds remaining idle. These percentages have reference to the computation shown in appendix III (p. 25) of the GAO report.

On the other hand, if the loanable funds of a bank are relatively limited, obviously, the bank would request an immediate participation loan type which not only would return a greater yield but it would also permit it to assist a greater number of customers. This also is a desirable result from the standpoint of accomplishing the objectives of the Small Business Act. I am convinced 93

that on the average, banks have loaned to a higher percentage of deposits during the past few years than ever before. Under such circumstances, they would tend to favor the immediate participation plan.

In view of (1) the effect demonstrated above that participation incentives have had on increasing participation loans and decreasing the necessity for exclusive SBA financing, and (2) the increasing scarcity of funds at reasonable rates to meet worthwhile loan requests towards a healthier small business community, I cannot agree completely with the GAO conclusions.

The GAO report in projecting its estimate of cost assumes that the banks would continue to participate at the same rate and would continue. to service the same proportion of such loans. I do not believe that either of these assumptions is valid. As previously stated, the proportion of participation loans was dropping prior to this action. I believe it is logical to assume also that as the cost of money increases and other expenses tend to rise, banks are more reluctant to take on additional servicing responsibilities if they can be avoided. Further, it is customary for banks to receive a fee for servicing loans for insurance companies and other institutions. Consequently, SBA, in all probability, faced the prospect of taking over the servicing of a greater proportion of immediate participation loans. I, therefore, cannot agree that the projected cost by GAO is fair.

With reference to the retroactive application of the fee decision, the banks represented in the loans · outstanding at the effective date of the new fee arrangement, had cooperated in a good faith belief that the Government was allowing them the maximum consideration available in equity. Had

SBA not included them in the new fee arrangement, it would have been easy for them to conclude that, being the more cooperative and easiest sold on the program among the banking community, they had been used to the extent of their resources and that more attractive offers were subsequently to be made to the less cooperative banks. 

Here again, I emphasize the unmeasurable effects on the program rather than the measurable costwhich is the area for policy decision that cannot be evaluated through clerical audit computations.

On the other hand and from a more measurable standpoint, SBA faced the prospect of having these banks request that SBA take over the servicing which would be additional expense to this agency.

With still further reference to the effect of retroactively applying the reduced deferred participation fee, it should be noted that if a bank had deferred participation loans under two fee arrangements, it would tend to request early participation by SBA in those loans having the higher participation cost. This would have resulted in a further lessening of the deferred participation proportion.

The report also states that the 0.5-percent service fee produces fees that are disproportionately high in relation to the services rendered on longer loans. In support of this conclusion, it quotes cer- m tain testimony of mine to the effect that it is as difficult to process, close, and service a $40,000 loan as it is loans three or four times that size. I might add that it is also as difficult, in many cases, to process, close, and service a $10,000 loan as a $40,000 loan. I agree that the 0.5-percent rate may be greater than the actual cost of servicing some of the large loans. It is also true that the 

0.5-percent rate produces income less than the cost of servicing some of the small loans. Since there are many more small loans than large ones, undoubtedly, the original assumption was that the 0.5-percent rate would average out in an equitable manner. 

I would like to reemphasize that I believe there is great value to responsible, competitive viewpoints in audit reports, and we will continue to evaluate the participation fee arrangement as 

GAO recommends-giving primary consideration to program results in the interest of small business but with due consideration to probable cost of the program.

I have provided the Comptroller General with a copy of this letter. 

With kind regards, I am, Sincerely, (S) JOHN E. HORNE, Administrator.

GAO REPORT 

"Need for More Effective Use of Manpower in the Set-Aside Program-Partially Corrected 

"A joint set-aside program is carried out by SBA in cooperation with major military and civilian procurement agencies of the Government under section 15 of the Small Business Act (15 94.

U.S.C. 631). Under cooperative agreements with procuring agencies, SBA representatives are assigned to major procurement offices and, working with procurement office representatives, screen proposed purchases to determine whether small concerns can supply all or a portion of the required items or services at a reasonable price to the Government. Suitable procurements are then set aside, in whole or in part, to be awarded competitively to small concerns. "In our report to the Administrator, SBA, in

June 1962, on the review of selected procurement and technical assistance activities, we commented on SBA's requirement that individual Govern- =ment procurements be reviewed for set-aside purposes jointly by SBA and procurement office representatives and pointed out that this procedure resulted in unnecessary duplication of effort at several military procurement offices. We had - noted that, through joint efforts with SBA, certain military procurement offices had established satisfactory procedures and had acquired suffient know-how to make satisfactory set-asides without SBA participation in each such set-aside. “After we had questioned, during our review, SBA's procedures involving this duplication of effort, SBA adopted in January 1962, a revision in its set-aside procedures for one major procuring agency, the Department of the Army. Under the revised procedures, the detailed participation of SBA representatives in set-aside actions per- =taining to procurement of items costing between $2,500 and $50,000 will be curtailed. However, - procurement requests for items costing over $50,000 will continue to be reviewed jointly by SBA and the Army procurement office representatives. We expressed the belief that an extension of the revised procedures would be desirable for these and other procurements. "We recommended that, to obtain more effective use of SBA's procurement manpower and to provide for increasing the scope of the set-aside program, the Administrator, SBA, further revise set-aside program policies and procedures to allow those procurement offices having an effective small-business program and the necessary capabilities to set aside all suitable procurements on the basis of their own determinations. We proposed that the efforts of SBA representatives at these procurement offices be limited to evaluating the effectiveness of the set-aside program as conducted by the procuring agency and to reviewing procurements which have not been set aside. 

The time thus saved could be used to survey procurement offices which are not yet included in SBA's program but which show potential for a set-aside operation."

SBA COMMENTS ON GAO REPORT The Small Business Administration received from the U.S. General Accounting Office a report on "Review of Selected Procurement and Techni cal Assistance Activities, Small Business Admin istration" under date of June 19, 1962.

In this report, the recommendation was made by GAO that ". . . to obtain more effective use of SBA's procurement manpower and to provide for increasing the scope of the set-aside program, the Administrator, SBA, further revise SBA's set-aside program policies and procedures to allow those procurement offices having an effective small business program and the necessary capabilities to set aside all suitable procurements on the basis of their own determinations . . ."

To this end, SBA has constantly undertaken to improve its set-aside operations. In cooperation with the Department of Defense, military departments, and civilian agencies, SBA has continued its liaison activities and close cooperation to revise set-aside procedures so as to accommodate a unilateral set-aside program. 

Reactivation of the set-aside program for military construction was agreed to by the DOD in 

August 1960 and was implemented during the fourth quarter of that year. It was arranged that set-asides would be considered to have been initiated by SBA on each construction procurement ranging from $2,500 to $500,000. Procurements above that limit were to be screened on an individual basis.

In July 1961, SBA through its liaison representative to the DOD, proposed that all military procurements with a dollar value ranging from $2,500 to $50,000 would be considered by the services as though SBA had automatically initiated a joint set-aside action on them. 

The proposal was determined by DOD not to be feasible. However, cooperative arrangements were made for this type of "automatic" set-aside program to be initiated with the Department of the Army on a unilateral basis. 

The program called upon Department of Army contracting officers to consider that a unilateral set-aside had been initiated on all proposed pro- 95

curements within the indicated dollar amount.

At the request of SBA, in May 1962, this same procedure was subjected to a test operation in the Air Force and Navy. The trial period was set for four months and at the end of that time, each of the services was to report on adaptability of the procedure to the procurements at each of their respective departments. 

On December 18, 1962, the reports were reviewed by the DOD and the conclusion reached that this technique should not be made a mandatory requirement nor should it be placed in the Armed 

Services Procurement Regulations as requested by 

SBA. The DOD had advised the military departments and Defense Supply Agency, however, that the procedure may be used on an optional basis at any purchasing activity. The Navy and the Air 

Force have maintained that the administrative burdens created by this program are not warranted by the resultant number of additional contracts going to small businesses. The Army has found the program useful and has decided to continue it throughout the entire department. 

Thus, it will be readily seen that SBA's efforts to comply with the GAO recommendation have been positive and constructive; that SBA has initiated specific steps to adapt its set-aside procedures to accommodate set-aside actions without SBA participation in each such action. In view of the foregoing, SBA must continue to give direct attention to joint set-aside programs with the DOD and civilian agencies in order to effectuate the intent and purpose of sections 2 and 15 of the Small Business Act, which enjoins SBA to insure that a fair proportion of Government contracts be placed with small business. 96

Appendix I 

Business Loans Approved in Year 

Beginning on page 98 is a listing of the 5,953 business loans approved by SBA in 1962. 

A "C" following the loan amount indicates that the loan was approved and subsequently canceled in full during the year. In addition, 317 loans totaling $22,815,866 which were approved in prior periods were canceled during 1962.

In the case of participation loans, the interest rate listed is that applicable to the participating institution's share of the loan. By law, the rate on SBA's share is limited to a maximum of 512 percent. Where deferred participation loans carry a higher rate of interest, the maximum rate of 512 percent on the SBA share does not apply until the agency has purchased its share of the loan from the participating institution. 97
